.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Christmas Tree Not German. At this season it is pleasant to reflect that, notwithstanding the popular notion fostered since the days of the Prince Consort, the Christmas tree is not German after all. Long before the war I was assured by my reading that Scandinavian legends spoke of a mystical tree called Yggdrasil, typical of Time, with roots stretching in three directions—towards heaven, the underworld, and the home of frost. But even further back than those times a tree had been used ceremoniously. The Egyptians made -one of a palm branch with twelve leaves, which they plucked at the winter solstice to typify the completion of the year. By palm here is meant the calendar tree, which puts forth a fresh shoot monthly. This- was the Egyptian gift tree. Italy borrowed the custom from Egypt, using the fir tree instead of the palm. This was each December illuminated with burning candles in honour of the sun-god Saturn; and during the ensuing feast the green boughs were hung with small waxen figures representing mythological characters, which were eventually distributed among the little Roman children. Our ornaments are the Christian substitutes for the heathen Phcebus and his kind. However, my chief point is that in this as in so much else (as we are con- stantly discovering) the Germans are mere copyists, with a gift for ready adaptation and for obtaining credit at a cheap rate. True, our own children who watch the tree with delight and receive with glee the presents which hang from its boughs, care nothing about Norse legend or Egyptian myth.

The Star of Bethlehem. What about this? What was the mysterious light that forms so miraculous an element in the beautiful story of Nazareth? Was it one of those comets whose flash across the sky has been supposed in ages past to herald some great hap- pening in history, just as the advent of Halley's comet seemed to the superstitious historian to have an association with the invasion of Eng- land by William the Conqueror Or was it one of the new stars that spend a brief but brilliant life in the heavens through the crashing to- gether of suns and the release of the vast energy they enclose? These are points one used to be fond of discussing in the long bygones, and they cropped up once more a few years ago, as I well remember. SU;science has little to offer by way of guidance to a satisfactory decision. Mince Pie.

Last week I had occasion to refer to the former almost invariable appearance of a boar's head at Christmas repasts. This igain, accord- ing to Dr. Brewer, had its origin in that Scandinavian mythology from which, as we have already_ seen, more than one custom has filtered- into modernity. Freye, the god of Plenty, used to ride a boar, Gullinbursti, and at his 'festival, held at Yule-tide, one of the species was invariably sacrificed. In banquets given at the season by the old feudal chieftains to their fellow nobles, a boar's head used to form the principal dish, borne into the hall on a gold or silver platter by a "sewer," who voiced an appropriate carol to an accompani- ment of trumpets. By-the-way, an old and prominent member of the Manchester Literary Club, whom I met in a tramcar on Christmas Eve, reminded me of several local dialect words that are of Scandinavian origin. I have long taken keen interest in the subject—lout of course that is another story. Carolling.

There was comparatively little of this on Christmas Eve; indeed, if my own experience be at all common, there has this season been more of it in the churches than outside. I have previously intimated how severely such manifestations were repressed in the days of the Commonwealth, and as I write I have before me some verses on the subject by old Needham, the chronicler. The carol revived but slowly, as one gathers from a song perhaps about 100 years old, the writer of which bemoans the time

"When Christmas had its Christmas carols. And ladies' sides were hooped like barrels."

Meanwhile, carols have revived, and crinolineS have once more had their day and ceased to be. The mill girls in a manufacturing town in which I lived when the latter absurd fashion was at its height, insisted on being supplied with hoops measuring nin. round. That by-the- way. I have just turned up an old number of the "Musical Journal," containing an article on carols by the Rev. A. E. Sharpie, and I may just indicate the drift. Perhaps the primary point of interest in the old Christmas carols is that they haye enshrined many an antique legend, superstitious enough, but often of great poetic beauty. Legends have gathered with' special affection round the flight into Egypt and similar incidents in the early life of the Holy Child. In an old print of 1701, at the head of a sheet of carols, there is a representation of the stable at Bethlehem with the Holy Family, a fox, cow, sheep, raven, and a cock, each with a label proceeding from its mouth with an enclosed

remark in Latin! Comic Interlude at Royal Function. I am having a sort of holiday this week; it may be noted that I do not advert to any current local event. I made incidental allusion to the late Prince Consort, and as I think of his illustrious grandson, the reigning monarch of these realms, and of the serious accident from the effects of which he is happily recovering, I am reminded how His Majesty succeeded to the throne owing to the premature death of his brother. I have reported a few Royal functions, and represented a Halifax paper when the late Prince Albert Victor, then stationed with his regiment at York, came over to present new colours to the local militia. Leaving out of account the abounding jealousies and heart- burnings and the vulgar and pitiable exhibitions of snobbery inseparable from sach occasions, let me at least mention one unrehearsed and diverting incident. When His Royal Highness, the cynosure of all eyes, had taken his position at the saluting base, an enterprising photo- grapher suddenly appeared as if by magic, and coolly planted a tripod camera right in front of the plainly somewhat disconcerted Royal coun- tenance, apparently with the objert of (so to I put it monopolising the pose at the critical juncture. Readers must 'please bear in mind that the institution in question, quite elabor- ately provided for at all great functions now- adays, had not then entered upon the American- ising process that has so effectually revolution- ised it in this 20th century; and the boldness and evident determination of the pushful one excited emotions of horror, dismay and anger, according to the status and the responsibility of the ~beholder. The commanding officer, purple With rage and betraying other alarming symptoms of apoplexy, shouted orders to a subordinate officer to remove the intruder instantly; another, likewise mounted and gorgeously apparelled as if 'but slightly inferior in rank, galloped at the charge toward the offending one, who, unconscious alike of gie sensation he had created and of his impending doom, proceeded in workmanlike manner to

adjust his camera and to focus his lens; and k the immense concourse of thrilled spectators— divided in opinion as to whether the too venture- I some picture taker would be forthwith " pinked " with the drawn sword of the fiercely- moustached attacking horseman, or summarily shot by a file of infantry, and afterwards hanged, drawn and quartered—breathlessly and with bulging eyes awaited the issue. For those whose mind had involuntarily reverted to historic crises when Christian martyrs were "butchered to make a Roman holiday." this was in the nature of an anti-climax. It is true that the venturesome artist was uncere- moniously bundled bag and baggage from his position of prominence on the greensward to one so obscure that his identity was speedily lost in the throng of eager sightseers; but, so far from any order being given to "Off with his head," not a drop of blood was spilt, nor was a mir of the so 'grievously-offending cranium injured. •

The fall back of the Allied armies on Salonica opened up delicate questions, in. which the observance by King Constantine of the "benevolent neutrality" he had promised the Allies seemed frequently in peril. ===== 0002601_19160101_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
In Which German Frightfulness SuccLmbed to British Doggedness.

BY A WELL=KNOWN rusueisT.

As 1915 drew towards its close, Sir John Simon, his Majesty's Secretary of State for Home Affairs, came down to the ,House of, Commons with a bill that was destined, even under. conditions of truce, to revive the still smouldesing embers of party strife. It pre- posed, among other things, to enact that the session of 1916 should be assumed to have followed immediately on that of 1914. By such facile methods can the politician erase a year from his records. Impartial history is less ac- commodating, and, if anything be indelible at all, the past year has left a mark upon the story of human development that will never be erased. Unlike its immediate predecessor, the year opened amid the shock of battle. Never have the guns been silent, and never has there been a cnange in the dominating concern in the minds of all nations.

DO lives. In the Dardanelles floating mines lespatched some Allied battleghips, and the hiumph and the Majestic Were torpedoed ott allipoli. The battleship Goliath was sunk by I Turkish destroyer in the Dardanelles in May. The destroyer Lynx struck a mine in the North Sea in August, the greater part of the crew being saved.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WESTERN FRONT.

Wide as are the ramifications of the world- war, it is the Western front which presses most upon the attention of this country. It is, so to speaa, at our own doors, and in the opinion of many competent judges it must be the decisive theatre of war. The original German plan was branded as a failure in the opening months of the war. Rapid success was of its essence, and it was not secured. The thrust which was turned aside at the battle of the Marne has made no progress subsequently. There have been local inci- dents of varying importance, such as the British ,stroke at Neuve Chapelle; which, if it was xiOt the comtplete success it might have been, resulted in an important advance. ,Time and again our soldiers have covered themselves with glory, and the increasing and unmistakable superiority of Allied aircraft has, been a principal factor in the situation. Temporary gain accrued to the enemy from the contravention of international law by the use of poison gas. Retaliation in this mode of warfare has been forced upon us by mili- ,tary considerations; but the blame must ob- viously rest • with those who initiated it. Hopes were raised here at the end bf Sep- tember by a communiqué which appeared to indicate that the "great offensive," which had been a sort of Parousia to many, had. begun. The German line in Flanders and Champagne was attacked with vigour. Trenehes were captured, prisoners taken and a substantial advance recorded. Some of the extravagant hopes created were not fulfilled. The monotony of trench warfare was re- aumed after this pleasing interlude of vivacity. But time fights for the Allies, and every .day makes the German failure more apparent. For over sixteen months the command of the British forces in this theatre was in the hands of Sir John French. It was a gigantic task, and the "severs and incessant' strain to which reference was made in the official report when he yielded up his command in the middle of December was anything but a fiction. Sir John had done a great work well, and the announcement' that he was to receive a Viseou.nty and the command Of the forces at home—no sinecure when a new Army had just. been created—afforded a clear indication of how it was regarded by his King and country. General Sir Douglas Haig was announced as -his successor.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYALTY AND THE WAR.

No account of the fighting in France would be complete which did not mention the in- spiriting effect upon our troops of the visits paid by King George. Whenever his Majesty went across he was received with an enthusi- asm born of more than conventional loyalty. An untoward occutrence _marked the last of

these visits. His Majesty's fall to the ground, es a result of his horse being frightened, con- fined him to his room for a month, with dis-

tressing though not 'serious injuries. The oc- casion, regrettable though it was, had the pleasing effect of providing fresh demonstra- tions of popular. affection towards the Throne. The Royal Family has throughout followed the progress of the war with close interest. The Queen and Queen-Mother and Princess Mary have extended their patronage to many Of the special efforts evoked by its problems; and the Prince of Wales has been sharing the rigours and—though attached to the Staff— the perils of the war in France.- Prince Albert's naval duties have, unfortunately, been interrupted, by illness. His Majesty's close personal interest in affairs was indicated very strikingly in what has beCOme known as "the King's Pledge." As. an example of war- time abstinence to his subjects, it was an- nounced that no alcoholic liquor would be consumed at the Royal Household during the war. Led by the Archbishop' of Canterbury, the bulk of the clergy imposed a similar' ordi- nance upon themselves, and it has been widely followed in the country.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE EAST.

Our eyes have been turned with admira- tion,- and sosietimes with anxiety, •on the efforts of our great Eastern Ally. Pessi- mists have looked sometimes with despair. But such despair arose from a • failure to grasp the realities of the situation. "Rus- sian retreat" looked' ominous to those who did not understand, what was the real aim of the Austro-German offensive. Between the melting of the snows and the beginning of the Russian winter it was•the design of the enemy, as Lord Kitchener put it, to destroy the Russian Army as a force in being. It was not achieved. There was a rapid ad- vance. The fall of Przemysl and the crossing of the Dniester were followed, despite the intervention of Italy, by the Capture of Lublin, Ivangorod, and Warsaw. Kovno. Georgievsk, and Brest-Litovsk fell, and it was clear that hopes of 'anything like an early Russian foffensiie must be set aside. But thanks to, the consummate strategy of our Ally the Army remained intact. Following their traditional method, the Russians took care that nothing of value was left behind ,for the invaders. Enthusiasm throughout the Czar's Empire reached its climax • when the Emperor himself took command of his forces and the Grand Duke Nicholas was appointed Viceroy and Commander-in-Chief in the Caucasus. Winter, and with it the cessation of all but local efforts, came with the German aim unattained.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
ITALY'S INTERVENTION.

Reference has been mad.e to the interven- tion of Italy. It was declared to be imminent for many months, but internal political affairs delayed it. In peculiarly difficult circumstances of mountain fighting Italian soldiers have done great work. They directed their activities towards a concentration on the north-east frontier to attack in the direction of Trentino, King Victor taking supreme command. Large forces of Austrians were detained to the advantage of Russia at a critical time, and Italy has since rendered direct aid in the Balkans.- At a time when Balkan Affairs were par- ticularly perplexing, and, the situation at the Dardanelles uncertain, Lord Kitchener,- went on an extended mission in the Near East, in- terviewing, among others, the King of Greece.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NAVAL WAR.

No discriminating person needs to be minded that, for all the valour of our "con- temptible little Army," our principal contri- bution to the Allied cause has been that of the Navy. It has been a silent work,. for the Ger- man Fleet has remained "bottled up" Kiel harbour. An attempt to repeat the in- famous shelling of undefended. towns resulted in. a. chase of the German squadron by Sir David teatiy's'squadron. The cruiser Blucher was sunk and two other cruisers were damaged. A minor damage to the Lion was all we paid for this victory, which taught a valuable lesson to the enemy. The German cruiser Dresden was demolished in the Pacific in March, and the last of the raiders, the Konisberg, was accounted for by our monitors. The year 'opened badly for us with the torpedoing of the Formidable in the Channel with a loss of about

creditable plots in winch .the implication of Austrian and German official representatives has been proved, and in regard to which more august persons are suspected, has not im- proved American feeling. The recall of Dr. Dumba and of Boy-Ed and von Papen for complicity in these plots was peremptorily de- manded by President Wilson with the country behind him. The President has, by common consent, been extremely patient.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PIRATES' CAMPAIGN.

What enemy naval activity the year has witnessed has been of a discreditable kind. The so-called submarine blockade was mere piracy, and unsuccessful paraey to boot. 'it' caused inconvenience at first, and. several fishermen were murdered. But our -resource- ful Navy soon foiled a way of-dealing with the situation.. The best submarine work has been done by our. own craft, in the sea of llittarmora, where, their activities •created terror in Con- s,tantinople. But the most • appalling ,deve/op- ment of German .lawlessness and 'brutality has been' in the attack on. passenger liners, of which the outstanding example renrains the Lusitania crime. This occurred on May 7th off the Old Head of Kinsale. The great liner was torpedoed' without warning, regardless of the fact that it was crowded with non-comba- tants, with their wives and children, or of the presence of many American, citizens on board. I No less than 1,131 lives were lost in this tragedy.

Those to ourselves have dealt with those points of international law which were bound to arise. With Ger- many and Austria, matters of greater moment have been raised. ===== 0002601_19160101_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
INEFFECTIVE FRIGHTFULNESS.

German frightfulness has had the two dis- tinguishing marks of brutality and ineffective- ness. Our own esperience of it has been mainly in the form of Zeppelin raids.. London and the East Coast suff.red several of these visits with considerable Icss of life and injury to persons,: but with nesligible material dam- age., • In copqueied -Belgium the G4rinan system has been seen at its worst, but even in such a chamber of horrors the case of Edith Cavill de- mands a special mention. The charge was one of assisting British soldiers to escape from .The hour of trial was carefully con- cealed from the American and Spanish Am- bassadors, and the gallant lady was shot in cold blood before there was any possibility of intervention. "A brutal murder under legal forms" it has been justly called. As though unwilling to be outdone in brutality, the Turks have carried out massacres of Armenian Christians on an unprecedented scale.

PRANKS AND FOLLIES OF THE PAST.

BY ARTHUR MOUNFIELO.

Lots of things remain to be told about Christ- mas and New Year, notwithstanding all that has been said and written. _

To begin with; it is a- mistake to suppose that Christmas is 1915 years old. In a sermon at Antioch on December 25th in the year 386, Chrysostom said: "It is not ten years since this day was clearly known to us." In other words, Christmas as an institution on December, 25th only came to be observed at the end of the fourth century. As a matter of fact, •it was not the usage of the Jews or early Christians to specially recognise birthdays. The death and resurrection of Jesus were commemorated, and at the right period of year, but, curiously enough, no tradition seems to have lingered as to His birth-time. Some placed it on April 20tli;,and some on May. 20th.' In Egypt they toole•a date in January: For a long time the Eastern Church took January 6th, the date now observed as Epiphany. The fact that shepherds were with their flocks at the time of the Nativity precludes the idea of a date in tinter, and beyorid a guess that it may have been in September or October, no one can offer any real WHY DECEMBER 25th WAS CHOSEN.

The choice of mid-winter as the date of Christ- mas will be known to most of our readers. December 25th comes near to the Winter Solstice, the turning point of the year, when the days lengthen, the sun begins his return to power. Almost all the ancient peoples kept a festival at this period. The Celts and Germans held extravagant orgies. The Norsemen held the great Yule Feast in cqmmemoration of the fiery sun-wheel, and believed that from Decem- ber 25th to January 6th they could trace the operations and movements of their deities, Odin, Berchtai &c. The Christian missionaries found it a hope- less task to uproot these old usages. The mid- winter feasting was .as much a part of their social life as Christmas is of ours. They fol-

===== 0002601_19160101_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUMMERS.

If the Christmas log was Baal-fire taken from the Norsemen, there was taken over from the Romans in early days a ,usage which . was destined to last through many Christian cen- turies, if indeed it does not linger yet. At the! Roman Saturnalia there was a practice of mas- querading, which gave great amusement. Men and women; dressed in each other's clothes- and. wearing' masks, went to each other's houses to ' play pranks and practical jokes. Against these fooleries the Christian leaders launched vigorous protests, but not with success. It was found better to try to transform the old usages and religious plays were organised to fill up the holidays, but " mummering," as it came to be called .in England, refused to be suppressed, and, long after Rome and her ways had been for- gotten it flourished as a Christmas pastime. It became an organised diversion, and corn- panics of mummers kept Outfits for their per- formances. In the Middle Ages professional bands of performers appeared before Royalty, and good companies often stood high in royal and aristocratic favour. Though practised on other occasions, it was essentially a Christmas habit. At the Reformation Henry VIII. issued an ordinance against " mumming and guising," declaring that all who went about to great houses arranged in this fashion would be liable te, arrest. as vagabonds and sent to gaol for 3 'months.' But the threat took little .effect. As none of our readers are ever likely to meet a party of mummers, and as none are old enough to have seen the sport in all its glory, it may be explained that the performers went out to Make Christmas fun in the most effective ways that could be devised. The young men dressed in the most gorgeous and ludicrous dresses, several of them being in character. They played a species of drama, based on the legend of George and the dragon, with many whimsical asides and abundant byplay. One of the chief figures was that of the dragon, a beast- of frightful mien and horrible appearance. St. George rode an imitation charger, and there were desperate, doings between the two. A doctor, with large wig and larger box of pills, was one of the crew, presumably to care for the wounded. An imitation village beadle kept order, a Turkish knight carried a huge sword, Father Christmas carried a wassail bowl, which served as a collecting dish, and the noisy crowd passed from house to house. In the play St. George took the field and challenged the world:—

What man or mortal will dare to stand Before me with my sword in hand; slay him and cut him as small as flies,

And send him to Jamaica to make mince pies

• The Turkish Knight (reminiscent of the Crusader) took up the challenge; but was slain after an awful encounter. St. George called for a doctor, and the physician appeared with the announcement :

I've a little bottle in my pocket Called hokum, shokum, alicampane; I'll touch his eyes, nose, mouth and chin, And say "Rise, dead man," and he'll fight again.

The Turk came to life and fought again with St. George even more vigorously, but there was another call for the doctor and the hokum shokum had to be applied once more to the prostrate Saracen.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LORD OF MISRULE.

In the great households the season was handed over to one who was chosen the "Lord of Misrule." He was appointed to lead and direct the revels of the season, and his reign lasted till Twelfth Day or January 6th; A knowledge of the doings and powers of this individual will convey some idea of the complete way in which the great houses were given over to, merriment during the Christmas season. The Lord ,pf Misrule was formally installed as the ruler of the household. Ile was attired. in mock

royal robes, and a page waited upon him. He entered .upon his duties by informing all the members of the household, in solemn conclave assembled, that he :absolved them of all their wisdom. They were allowed to retain just enough sense to enable them to make fools of

them-selves, and no more. No one was to pre- sume to be wise enough to laugh at others. As Lord of the hour he solemnly placed a magic spell over them to change them into children once more, and insisted that until Twelfth Day they should act as such. - -

The members of the house all swore loyalty to their new lord, and the fun began. Richard Evelyn, the father of John Evelyn. drew up a -document in mock seriousness for his'Lord of Misrule at his estate at Wotton.

Imprimus, I give free leave to Owen Flood, my trumpeter; gentleman, to be Lord of Mis- rule of all good orders during the twelve days. And also, I give free leave to the said Owen Flood to commalid all and every person or persons- whatsoever, as well servants as others, to be at his command whensoever he shall sound his trumpet or . music, and to do him good service as though I were present my- self at their peril. . . . I give full power and authority to his lordship to break up all bolts, locks, bars, doors and latches, and to fling up all doors out of hinges to come at those who presume to disobey his lordship's commands. God save the King! In the Stuart 'period the habit was carried to indecent lengths. It became a town and village habit, much to the unhappiness of good folks, for not even in Church were they free from the noise of riot. Stubbes, the Puritan, wrote:—

All the wilde heads of the Parishe chuse them a capitaine of ;mischief whom they in- noble with the title of my Lorde of Misrule, and hym they crown with great solemnitie and adopt for their kyng. This being anoynted chuseth for the twentie, fortie, three- score or a hundred lustie guttes like to hym- selfe, to waite uppon his Lordly Majestic, and to guard his noble persone. Then every one of these his menne he investeth with M., liveries, of green, yellowe or some other light , wanton colour. And as if they were not handle enough they bedeche themselves - with scarffes, ribons and laces hanged all over with gold rings. Thus all things set in order then have they their hobble horses,, dragons and other antiques, together with their handle pipers and thunderyni.r, drommers to strik2 ur the Devilles Dance withall, then march these heathen companie towards the Church and the Church yaide, their pipers pipyng, their drommers thonderyng, their stumppers daunceyng, their belles jyngling, their hobbie horses, and other monsters skirmishyng amongest the throng r and in this sorte they goe to the, Church, though the minister bee at praier or preachyng, daunevng in the Church• like Devilles incarnate with such a confused noise that no man can hear his owne

Of the wonderful dishes they prepared, the Christmas pie made famous by Jack liorlier and the greater doings of the Twelfth Night we should like to add something, but these things must wait for another chapter.

THE DARDANELLES.

The time has not yet come when the attempt to force -the Dardanelles can be viewed in its proper perspective. The attempt arose out of Russia's need of munitions, and it was hoped that by this great feat—rendered possible, as some thought, by such sea monsters as the Queen Elizabeth—we might open the passage to Russian ports on the Black Sea. The original endeavour was to force a passage by purely naval operations, and they began on February 19th. But that design was later abandoned, and what Mr. Churchill calls "a joint amphibious coup de main" was at- tempted'. The story since then has been one of fierce fighting amid terrible conditions. The story of the landings is the story of almost 'un- parallelled gallantry, but unfortunately not of success, and despite the gallantry of our sol- diers, especially the Colonials, at first under Sir lan Hamilton and afterwards under Sir Charles Monro, a withdrawal was carried out in December, fortunately with slight loss, from the positions at Suvla Bay and Anzac.. THE BALKAN TANtLE: The Balkan States have always been a riddle and a storm centre to Europe, but never have their affairs been more perplexing than during the past year. The broad fact' about the Balkans during 1915 has been that the Central Powers aimed at creating division in that part of the-world, and the -diplomacy of the Allies was directed to the re-establishment of the Balkan League. The. former was, of course, much the easier task., Bulgaria was persuaded to come ,in on the side, of the Austro Germans---thus ranging herself in alliance with Turkey -and against Russia; a monstrosity in the eves of -any -student of history. An attack oè Serbia, who could thus be stabbed in the back, followed, an the suf- ferings of that country have- been appalling. The attitude of Greece has throughout , been perplexing. Vernzelos, the Greek Premier, speaking, as elections proved, for the country, made overtures 'to, the Allies, based, on the fulfilment by Greece of her tieaty obligations to Serbia. But the Premier NV 88 repudiated . _ •

dated. The formation of a Coalition Govern- ment, with Mr. Asquith 'at its head, last June was a. historic step. All parties were repre- sented. Idleness and Activity.

-- Laziness grows on people—it begins in cob- webs, and ends in iron chains. The more work one has to do in this world, the more he is able to accomplish, for he learns to econanaise his time.

Man's By-products. There is enough hydrogen gas in a man, says the "Medical Index Lancet," to carry him up to the clouds. He contains enough fat to make 75 candles and a large cake of soap, and enough phosphorus to make 8,064 boxes of matches. His remaining constituents will yield, if utilis-ed, six cruets of salt, a bowl- of sugar and 10 gallons of water.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
lowed the wiser plan of appropriating them. Gradually the coarse things were checked, and the days which used to be filled with riot, were .filled by religious dramas. Dramatic representa- tions of ' the birth of Christ were used as a means of teaching. Special syinbblical food, such as mince pies made in the form of a man- ger, became the custom. Manger songs and carols were written, and the people were taught to spend their raptures over the glad event of the /coming of Christ. The gifts of the Wise Men suggested, gifts to the Church and to each other.

Many of the things we regard as distinctly Christian were in the world, long before the Christian era. The Christmas tree is an in- stance. In the Roman Empire mid-winter was the time of Saturnalia, and those who have read Virgil will find a distinct picture of our Christ- mas tree, with its pendant toys and dolls—. Oscilla ex alta suspenduunt mollia pinu. No one will complain that December 25th was chosen as the date of the celebration of the Nativity of Jesus. Like our remote ancestors, we find that good cheer comes most acceptably when,days are dull and the nights are long. Nor will anyone complain that the Church took over ancient holidays and institutions. What if our holly and mistletoe are the laurel of the Saturnalian Feast, and the Yule Log the sacri- fice to Baal! They are the more interesting' and not less on that account.

And what objection can be urged against tak- ing twelve days of carnival and crowding them with sacred plays and carols and, incident:My, with Christmas pies? _ _

For the purpose of an article or two we may be allowed to set forth a few disconnected gleanings which belong to the twelve days that join Christmas with Epiphany.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDERMASS.

The day which followed Christmas Day was Childermass, and celebrated Herod's murder of ,the infants. It was regarded as a day of most unlucky omen. For anyone to marry on that day would have been regarded as little short of madness. It was cb nsidered to be un- lucky for the commencement of any labour or enterprise, but as the day fell immediately after the Christmas rejoicings, it ,is not likely that ill-luck was often incurred on this account. In

the monasteries they regarded the day of the week on which Childermass fell as unlucky all through the year, and whatever the day it was carefully watched each week as the- possible bringer of misfortune. The unhappiness fell chiefly upon the children. In Gregoirv's " Posthuma," written in 1649, we are told, "It

Make your OLD Grates into NEW

hath been a custom, and yet is elsewhere, to whip the children upon Innocents' Day morn- ing, that the memory of Herod's murder of the innocents might stick the closer, and in a moderate proportion to act over the crueltie again in kinde." It hardly needs to be said that our forerunners had their own ideas on the sub- ject of memory training.

AGRICULTURAL RELIEF OF ALLIES.

Sir,—ln the autumn we appealed for seed wheat, which received a generous response. We

now appeal for spring oats to assist the ruined farmers in the areas devastated by the war. The average area sown with oats in the devastated districts of the Marne and the Meuse, is 531,250 acres, and in the department of Aisne and Meurthe over 400,0.00 acres. My committee appeal for gifts of spring seed oats. white or black, and ask donors to be good enough to state what they are willing to give not later than January 21st next. Instructions as to forwarding will be sent early in February. The seed oats will be dispatched by Government transports; free of charge, to the other side, and on Arrival there- will be taken over by the French Government and distributed to the farmers most in need of them. G.-NORRIS MIDWOOD, Chairman of Seeds, Cereals and Miscellaneous Committee. 16, Bedford-square, London, W.C.

:1I TEAL TO ROMAN CATHOLICS.

Sir,—Will you allow me through the medium of your well-known paper to appeal to the Roman Catholics of Warrington for comforts for their co-religionists from that town (who are .twenty in number), who are in the 7th Battalion Royal Inniskilling Fusiliers, which is Under orders to go, very shortly, to the Front? This Regiment is almost entirely composed of Roman Catholics, and being an Irish .Regiment, officered by Irishmen who are. mostly poor men, there are very few of. them able to afford sub- scriptions large enough to fulfil the men's requirements. The following are the articles demandmostlyin -

.... . Mufflers,. in grey or khaki, about 52 by 12 inches. Mittens, without thumbs, but with holes for thumbs, S or 10 inches long. Socks, 11 to 111 inches long. Warm flannel shirts, in grey or khaki. (No - flannelette). Cardigan jackets.. Tobacco, cigarettes and pipes. Checolates, biscuits, Meat lozenges, sweets. Insect powder, soap. Paper, envelopes, indelible pencils. Khaki handkerchiefs. Light literature, magazines (new); or Money to provide any of the -above list at wholesale Prices when possible. Mr. B. Cun- ningham, of Warrington, has most kindly given me 'a generous,donation of £5. I shall be glad to receive any of the above articles or money, and will forward them to the Colonel's wife, Mrs. Michael Hughes.

Parcels addressed to Newton-k-Willows Station will be acknowledged by me, also money to purchase goods, which can be-sent to the above address. I. venture to express a hope that the Roman. Catholics of Warrington will be very kind and help me to send out fortnightly parcels to those men who have so gallantly gone from Warrington to defend their homes and country. Any donation, however small, will be most gratefully reeeived. I remain, clear Sir, Yours very truly, MARY LAURA GERARD (The Dowager Lady Gerard). Carswood Hall, Newton-le-Willows, Lancashire, 28th December, 1915. P.S.—Kindlv write outside each parcel "For Inniskilling Fusiliers."

SALVATION ARMY SOCIAL ACTIVITIES.

_Sir,—The stormy year just drawing to a close. has been one of no little anxiety, says General Booth, in his report on the social operations of the Salvation Army, and strain on that branch. of the work. There has been an unusual pres- sure upon the resources of our friends owing to the war, and there has been a very serious corresponding difficulty within the circle of oui. own activities from the fact that so much of the ordinary business of the community has been Suspended and labour deflected into un- usual channels. Many departments of the. scheme . have ,nevertheless been working well. We have on the men's side of the work, where difficulty has been most evident, -made every effort to maintain the framework of our organisation as a whole, but this, of course, hag involved certain expenditure which in the nature of the case has been more than usually remunerative.

Buildings have to be paid for whether they are Occupied or not. Land ought to be culti- vated—be labour ever so .scarce. .

On-, the ,women's side of the work, the special circumstances of the time have affected' us rather differently. gaily women of good character, among them many dependents; of soldiers, as well as many of those who have lost . all, have been placed in very trying cir- cumstances, and over and over again .the Army's agencies have been utilised to prevent catas- trophies, or to help those in one kind of trouble or another to recover what they have lost. - During the year the Salvation Army has sup- plied nearly seven million meals at the cheap Food Depots, and over 230,000 cheap lodgings .for the homelec;s. 1,242 ex-criminals have been assisted, restore‘d to friends . or put in situa- tions. 380 ex-criminals have been received in our homes.

During the year the special department for dealing with le_st. persons .has received 1,505 applications, involving inquiry concerning those who are missing, of these 316 have been found through the special 'agency of the department. It is interesting to note that since this valuable agency has been in existence the Salvation Army has been able to recover 13,660 persons who had in various ways been lost of their friends.

The whole of this work is entirely dependant upon voluntary gifts, and General Booth states that at least g 48,000 is required in order that the Salvation Army may be able to continue its social wok during the next financial year. In order to remove misconception, it may be stated that the accounts relating to the Army's Social Funds are kept separate from those 'of the ordinary work, and that balance sheets may be obtained upon application to the Secretary, 101, Queen Victoria-street. E.C. T. H. KITCHING. Fereign Office, Colonel. Queen Victoria-street, London, E.C.

BELGIAN BENEDICTINE.

Distinguished Ecclesiastic At The Hippodrome.

On Sunday evening, at the Hippodrome, was delivered to an appreciative audience a lecture on' the Holy Land by the Right Rev.. Abbot Janssens, a gentleman renowned as an able speaker, and one who has witnessed the horrors .of the present war in Belgium, his native land. • The Right Rev. Abbot Janssens was born at a small village named Saint Nicholai, midway between the towns of Termonde and Louvain. At the outbreak of war he was in his native village and witnessed the hordes of Germany creating wanton destruction in those towns, now nothing more or less than a heap of ruins.•" The vandalism of the enemy was terrible to witness," said the lecturer, all that remains •of those once beautiful churches are piled up stones and pieces of glass." The right reverend gentleman is a man whom physiognomists would like to 'meet. He .has a fine forehead, eyes blue as the skies, a brow wrinkled with thought and voice tender end .pathetic, yet at times, when describing the scenes he has witnessed, strong and fervent. He speaks with a pronounced foreign accent which, ,at ,times, is not quite clear, specially when expressing himself on the sacrileges of the Common foe. Ofttimes there .was pathos in his voice and regret at his country's untold loss yet, withal, his is a philosophic nature, for he has the belief that from the ashes of his beloved lend, a truer and deeper religion will be wit- nessed in the hearts of his countrymen. As already stated, his lecture opened by .his show- ing the ruins of his .own country, and from there we had the pleasure of witnessing.many beauti- ful scenes in foreign lands. First one seemed to be under the blue skies of Italy, at. Milan, sailing along the beautiful harbour of Brindisi. Next one was transported. along the Mediterranean to Corfu. Athens, Con- stantinople and Jaffa, life in the. Turkish capi- tal, all taken first hand, being very vivid and impressive.

Views of the .Accropolis were Particularly fine and were thoroughly enjoyed by the audience. A picture of Stamboul, with mosques and minarets practically made one think one was in the heart of this Oriental suburb, so splendidly was it portrayed on the screen. From there the tour which the right reverend gentle- man made, proceeds to the Holy Land, spots sacred and associated with the life and passion of Jesus Christ.

Particularly impressive were the viewgoof the Mount of Olives, the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusa- lem. Nazareth, Mount Tabor and the Sea of Galilee.

Next one saw the sights of Egynt, the Pyramids and Spinx. at sunset. and the Nile placid and motionless. _

- • At this noint the lecturer commented on the British " Tommies" stationed in this Eastern land. and said that a few years ago be did not think he would become so intimate with the defenders of our Empire: but. said he. "Every time I now see an English soldier I always feel a special sentiment."

The lecture concluded with a brief visit to Rome and the showing of His Maje-ty the King. "Father Jackson, on behalf of the audience, thanked the Right Per. Abbot Jans:.ens for his kindness in visiting Warrington. and said that he felt sure every member of the audience had thoroughly enjoyed the instructive and educa- tional lecture, which would be remembered in the days to come. Ile also thanked the management- of the Hippodrome for so tenerously • placing the spacious hall at their disposal, and the staff for giving•their services.

The nroceeds of the lectnrp oro to be devoted to St. Benedict's Church, Orford-lane.

WORKS FATALITY,

Lad Falls Down A Trap-Door At Rylands's.

The sad story of a boy's death was related at an inquest held by Mr. S. Brighouse, at the Warrington Police Station on Tuesday after- noon. The inquiry was on the body of Patrick Lane, aged 14 years, who resided at 22. Gandy- street, and who died as the result of falling through a trap-door at Messrs. Rylands Bros. Works, Church-street. Mr. T. S. Steel (for Messrs. Henry Greenall and Co.) appeared for the employers, and Mr. E. A. White for the relatives of the deceased. Mr. H. R. Brothers, his Majesty's factory inspector, and Mr. Wads- worth, works manager for Messrs. Rylands' Bros., were also present. Mary Lane, a widow, living at 22, Gandy- street, said that the deceased washer son. He went to his work on the previous Thursday morning at six o'clock. At eleven o'clock he was .brought home in a cab, and she was in- formed by the workmen who accompanied him that he had fallen through a trap-door, and that he had had his head bandaged at the In- firmary. He was nnconscious, and looked very ill. She sent for Dr. Liston, who came and at once ordered his removal to the Infirmary. He was taken there in an ambulance. He died the same day at 6.45, p.m. He bad worked at Messrs. Rylands's for twelve months.

Mary Allen, a sister at the Infirmary, aid that when admitted, the boy was suffering from a fractured skull. An operation was per- formed, but he died on Thursday evening. The Coroner: You don't know why he was sent home and then carried back to the place from whence he came? The Witness: No, sir.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
Over-balanced Himself.

John Lawless (13), 6, Platt-street, said he was employed. in the warehouse, and at 9.55 on Thursday morning the foreman came to him and two :other boys, and told them to go to the hoist to get some wire up. He went to the warehouse floor, *here lie found a youth named Edward Dykes. Dykes got on one of the trap doors, and Lane stood with one fc.ot on one door and his _other foot partly on the other door. Lane was looking down through the hole which the chain passed through. Dykes lifted one of the doors and Lane over-balanced himself and fell through the hole. Dykes shouted for the chain to be lowered, and then slid down it to Lane's assistance. Witness ran to the fore- man and told him what had happened. The Coroner: Why. did Dykes lift one of the doors?

Witness: I think it was to slide down the chain to save going round. Edward Arnold Dykes, a general labourer, stated that at 9.55 on the Thursday morning he was engaged rolling wire across the yard to the warehouse. He rolled it all into position below the hoist and then went to the warehouse to tell the foreman that it was ready to go up. The foreman sent Lane and another boy to help and they all three went to the trap. Lane and the witness got on one of the doors to lift the other up, so as to let the boy below know that they were ready. When the door had been lifted Lane reached over to a piece of wire to fasten it, and in so doing overbalanced himself and fell through the aperture on to the rolls of wire in the yard below. Witness im- mediately slid down the chain to his assistance, and helped to carry him to the lodge. The Coroner: You were not lifting uP the doors to slide down the chain? Witness: No, -sir. The Coroner: How many times have you lifted up the door?

Witm:s;l: I could .not say; many a time. Questioned by Mr. White, the witness said he had no object in sliding down the chain, because he had just gone up. In reply to Mr. Steel, he denied that it was necessary for him to go to the bottom of the hoist again. He had slid down the chain many times, but his employers did not know this. James Hickman, also a general labourer, who was at the bottom of the hoist, said that he expected Dykes to •come down after he had finished his message. In reply to Mr. Brothers, the witness said it was not necessary to open the doors. He ex- pected Dykes to come down by the chain.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
To Save Time.

William O'Neill, the foreman, statA that it would take a person five minutes to get,to the bottom by the ordinary way, but by sliding down the rope he could get there in a couple of seccinds: .That would be done to save time. The Coroner commented that "Lads will be lads," and..that very probably he would have done the same thing when he was a lad. - The jury returned ,a. verdict of "Accidental death," and suggested that a bell should be in- troduced to give the necessary signal. • The Coroner said be would rpave that matter in the hands of his Majesty's inspector. •

Aikin-street.

Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest on Tuesday afternoon at the Police Station upon the body of Mrs.. Ann Backhouse, 69 years of age, of .12, Aiken-stieet, Warrington. Mrs. Alice Bebbington, 28, Garibaldi-street, a daughter of the deceased, stated that for the last nine years her mother had resided in Aiken- street, but had taken her meals at the residence of the witness. She saw her mother twice later in the day, and on ,both occasions she seemed all right. At nine o'clock at night the witness was told that her mother had been found dead.

Found on the Stairs.

Mrs. Mary Ann Naylor, who lived with Mrs. Backhouse, said that on Saturday morning three of them had u gill of whisky among them in their tea. Later they had a gill of beer each. Witness went out and left Mrs. Backhouse lying on the sofa. When she returned the woman was lying en the stairs. Witness thought that she had been overcome by the beer. Assistance was secured and the wqman was lifted on to the sofa in the kitchen and was left there. Later in the day when she found the woman still lying there, she felt at her hand and found that it was cold. It was then found that the woman was dead.

The Opportunity for a Bomestic Jumble Sale. The war has 'brought to light many hidden things, and encouraged little economies. It has made scarce articles that were formally con- signed to the dustbin, such as empty bottles, glass and stoneware jars. Housewives who have made a point of storing up these articles may now reap their reward, and realise a fair sum by disposing of them. Old string may also be turned into money, as to buy it new is twice as expensive as it was, in consequence of the diffi- culty in obtaining Russian and Italian hemp. There is also a good demand for second-hand furniture and clothing; owing to money having become common among classes where it used to be uncommon. The fabric of discarded evening gloves, and the upper part of worn-out silk stockings may be made useful. Altogether, there never was such an advantageous time for the housewife to clear out kitchen, scullery, cellar, attics, wardrobes, all probably encum- bered with the accumulations of years, if not generations, and of little or no monetary value in normal times.

The Menace of the Crinoline.

Paris is sending forth new modes and models, almost as usual, but the women of the coun- tries at war will neither adopt the one nor wear the other, for even if they had the desire, oppor- tunities for showing off fine clothes are few and far between. Throughout the Witish Isles women who before the war followed the chang- ing fashions most scrupulously, are wearing their last year's suits and gowns with the utmost sangfroid. Therefore the narrow skirt and the long straight coat have not disappeared, although both pre now quite out of fashion. But new modes give employment to designers, and their workpeople, who needs must live, therefore Paris is sending its models to the 'United States and the South American Repub- lics, where there is a good market for such wares. A noticeable feature of the full short skirts sent to these countries is their inflation by means of an apparently harmless line of stiffening, which is said surely to herald the advent of the crinoline.

Then mike into a stiff batter with milk (into which a well- beaten egg may be added), roll out about an inch thick and cut into triangles, and make in a quick oven. A Sally Lunn is made thus :—Mix together two teaspoonfuls of baking soda and four of cream of tartar, add two table- spoonfuls of powdered sugar, and 21b. of fine flour. Oatmeal Cakes. Put 11b. of oatmeal into a basin with a little salt; melt a large teaspoonful of dripping in a cupful of boiling water, and stir in enough to the meal to make a soft paste. Mix with the hand, turn out on to a board, dusted with meal, divide into two portions, roll out to the eighth of an inch thick, rub over with more dry meal, and cut into four ," farles." Slip these on to a hot girdle and bake slowly till the edges curl slightly; then take them from the girdle, and toast the upper side before a clear fire.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MiARJORIE.

The Care of Birds.

A pretty Christmas custom prevails in Den- mark, which Queen Alexandra has never, failed to Put in practice since first she came amongst us. Every farmer's wife in Denmark hangs a wreath of corn over her front door on Christ- mas morning; in order that the birds may also , enjoy a feast on that Holy Day. Few people realise how much pleasure and enjoyment they derive from the song birds, for which our. country has always been renowned, and multi- tudes of singing birds visit these shores even. year—some from very distant lands. Thanks to the many societies that have sprung up in recent years, bird baths, feeding tables, and nesting boxes are now found in many gardens, and much more protection for bird life is enforced by law. Curiously enough, the presence of one species often induces others to take. up their quarters in a garden, for there is a certain amount of fellowship among birds.

Hockey Players and Farm Work. - Women have everywhere taken up war work and achieved success in many unexpected direc- tions, but they have not, it is said, responded in a body to the country's 'heeds, from an agricul- tural point of view. In the South, women have long since ceased to work upon the land, although in ,the North the wives and daughters of farmers continue to take an active part in the work of the hay and corn harvests, and in the making of butter and cheese. Two ladies, however, who offered . thAir services for farm work through the Norwich Labour Bureau, have certainly risen to the Occasion, and are making first-rate farm bands. Both ladies before the war were well-known hockey players, and to their training: on the hockey ,grounds they attri- bute their ability to withstand the hard work and the most inclement weather. One of these ladies, Miss-Gowing, is the captain and honor- ary secretary of the Norfolk County Ladies' Hockey Club ;"Miss Coleman—the other lady—is a distinguished member of the same. Their work has included the tending and feeding of bullocks for the Christmas markets, carting roots, chaff cutting, mange! palling,: and Potato

110111 NOTES.

Profitable Recipes And Useful Suggestions.

Scrambled Eggs With Cheese.

Arrange four slices of fresh buttered toast on a hot platter, and over each slice pour a table- spoonful of hot milk. To four eggs add four tablespoonfuls of milk, four tablespoonfuls of grated mild cheese, a little salt, a few grains of pepper, and heat slightly. Melt a tablespoon- ful of butter in an omelet pan, and when hot turn in the mixture. Cook quickly and stir constantly, lifting up from the bottom of the pan until the contents are firm, but soft. Arrange the eggs on toast and serve. Garnish with sprigs of parsley. Potato Omelet.

Mix cold boiled potatoes cut in bits with half a cupful of soup-stock, and let them absorb it. Put them in a frying-pan with a piece of butter the size of an egg. When they begin to brown stir in two tablespoonfuls of grated cheese, and then let the mixture brown on one side, like an omelet. Serve on a flat dish, browned side up. Apple Pie.

Peel and • core the apples. Leaving them in quarters, put them, well piled up, into a pie- dish with sugar to your taste, with a few cloves, some thinly-cut lemon-peel, or stick cinnamon broken in strips. Pour over a tea- cupful of water. Make a puff or short paste, roll it out to about Sin. thick, cover the top o the pie, and bake for three-quarters of an hour in a good hot oven. If possible, use an apple which finds its own sugar—like the Blenheim or a good Reinette.

Apple Pudding. Make a nice dough with Ilb. of flour and Vb. of butter. Well butter a pudding-basin, roll out the dough to half an inch in thicknesS, line the buttered_ basin. Fill this in with peeled and cored apples cut in quarters, sugar to taste, flavoured with lemon peal, Cloves or cinnamon, and a cupful of cold water. Roll out a piece of paste to fit the top of the basin, tie a cloth over the top, and put it in a saucepan with just enough boiling water to reach halfway up the basin. Boil two hours if of medium size, three hours if large. , - •

Roll out quite thin, and divide into quarters, which must again be halved: Bake on a griddle on both sides and toast 'just before. serving. HINTS ON HEALTH. Useful Information In Popular Language.

Brown Bread for Children. If children are given brown bread from the first day that they eat bread and butter, they will be used to it and prefer it. Mothers must not be too lenient in allowing children to refuse brown bread and prefer white. In the same way, the average town-dweller likes to see his butter yellow because it reminds him of butter- cups, and so the fresh butter has to be dyed yellow.

Keeping the Baby From Colds. You can keep your baby from taking cold. Colds are both dangerous and unnecessary. Just because " everybody " in the neighbourhood is having a cold is no reason why your baby should likewise have one, though it must be admitted that this is more than likely the cause of his in- disposition at this season, for colds are con- tagious, particularly during the sneezing or coughing stage. If every home had a quarantine room to which the sick and and ailing members could be retired and made comfortable and happy, there would be no need of infecting the whole house- hold because one member .was ill with a cold or other contagious malady. _ How to Prevent Them.

in the matter of prevention there are other points to be considered. At this season, when the cold and chill tempts one to stay within doors, the little one is not infrequently deprived of much of the fresh oxygen he has been accustomed to during the warmer season, when outdoor living was the rule. He is kept in super-heated rooms, and he is over-clothed be- cause it is cold weather, even though it is a summer temperature in the rooms where he lives. He perspires in the warmth, and then if the outside door be opened the draught may give him a sudden chill. Perhaps at night, Or while taking his nap, he kicks off the covers and gets cooled off too much. Quite generally the reason for this is because he was at first too warm. Lessen the chances of a cold from these sources by clothing and covering the little ones in accord with the everyday atmosphere in which they live, but always provide for changes in temperature as they occui. Make certain the feet and hands are at all times warm.

Insure tht children against taking cold by training their skin from early babyhood with the daily cold bath. See that t-..ey breathe pure air night and day. Let them live in the sun- shine and be much out of doors. Keep the digestive organs sound and healthy by using good food and permitting proper demise: Babies allowed to follow their own bent are apt to take voluntarily all the exercis2 Nature calls for. unless they are hampered in some way:— E. E. Kellog, in "Good Health."

WATCHING A HORSE.

"Boy, watch my horse till I come back I" called a man to a boy, lounging around the station, as he hastened to bid farewell to a departing friend. "Certainly, sir," said the boy, taking the reins. Just then the locomotive whistled, and the horse, rearing suddenly, started at full speed up the 'road. The .130 started after the fleeing animal, and, as the owner appeared, exclaimed with relief: "It's a good, thing .you came now, sir, for I couldn't have watched him very much longer."

A GOOD BEAR STORY.

James Oliver Curwood, in " God's Country and the Woman," published by Messrs. Cassell, tells the following amusing story of a polar bear hunt :

"I had climbed up to the top of an ice mountain. It was, perhaps, two hundred feet high, and in the shape of an almost yerfect pyramid. At the very top was a block (af tce, possibly eight feet square, and puffing con- siderably, I sat down with my back avinst it, and gazed off over the Arctic ice-field.:. My companions were a biile away, and the polar bear we were after was nowhere in sight., "Suddenly, I heard a sound, and I leAr.c.l cver a little, and looked round the corner (.1' my ice- block. Gazing round the ,other corner, Hs breath almost in my face, was the head of, the biggest polar bear I had ever seen. That head, six feet away, looked as big as a wash tub. I had a gun across my knees, but I didn't Wait. I gave myself just one shovB, and off I Went down the sloping side of that ice-mountain.-- twisting, and somersaulting, and sliding the two hundred feet to the bottom, my rifle and .pipe and tobacco pouch travelling along behind me.

".1 reached the bottom' ali;'e. Iw-as so scared I hadn't felt a jar. I looked back, expecting the bear. But he was nowhere in sight. This fact revived my courage. I secured my gun, and made my way back to' the apex of the pyra- mid. And on the other side, half a mile away, I saw that polar bear running like grim death. My face, unshaven for two moilths, had scared him as badly as he had frightened me. He had torn up the snow and ice like a rock-slide in going down the pyramid. He was running when I last saw him, and from the momentum he had gathered I doubt if he has been able to stop yet. "So far as my knowledge goes, it's the first time a human face ever scared a polar bear, and I have been wondering ever since if it was a compliment to the manly strength and fear, 'sameness of my features or the other thing !"

===== 0002601_19160101_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
SUCCESS IN THE POULTRY YARD.

Incubators And Their Manage-

ment.

By E. T. BROWN.

Incubators are now no longer the toy that they were a few years ago. They have now be- come an absolute necessity on every modern poultry farm. If one is willing to work along the old ruts in the same old way one may dis- pense with their use. But to the man who intends to make his poultry pay they are a necessity. Winter hatching is essential to the attainment of successful financial results, and this is just the very time when broody hens are conspicuous by their absence. Even if an occasional hen is obtained during the winter it is unwise to trust her upon 'valuable eggs, be- cause upon the slightest provocation she will forsake and ruin them. The great advantage of an incubator is that it is always ready for work, and hatches as well in the depth of winter as the height of summer. The outside temperature has no effect upon it, and-thus no matter how cold the weather it will always do its work properly. A Reliable Machine.

This is, of course, provided that a reliable machine is selected. And there are several of these upon the market at the present time. The price of a good machine is by no means pro- hibitive; its cost is repaid by two or three suc- cessful hatches. But it is a greaz mistake tq buy too cheap a machine, for in this case indifferent material and workmanship must be expected. It cannot be otherwise. One of the recognised makes should be purchased: for they have been tried and proved. There is still, in some people's minds a prejudice against artificial hatching, as they imagine that chickens hatched artificially are neither so strong nor so healthy as are those brought out under a hen. This is quite a mistaken idea, and it has been proved over and over again that when birds ate attended to properly, and pro- vided with good brooders and suitable foods, they are equally hardy, and thrive equally well as those hatched in the natural manner. Every- thing depends upon the manner in which the birds are treated. If they are neglected, failure is bound to ensue; but this applies equally to the natural method. Another statement that requires to be contradicted is that incubators do not hatch as large a percentage of fertile eggs as does a hen. If the machine is carefully attended to by an experienced person, the hatch- ing results are just as successful. Where To Work The Machine.

There is no place so suitable in which to work an incubator as a cool, dry cellar. There is always an even temperature below the level of the ground, for heat and cold are atmos- pheric. In the U.S.A. where I have visited a large number of poultry plants, the incubator rooms are nearly always underground, but we do not experience the same degree of heat and cold in this country, and thus, unless a cellar happens to be available, it is unnecessary to excavate One specially for the purpose. Theor- etically, the outside temperature should have nothing to do with the inside heat of the in- cubator, but in practice we find that it has. Failing a cellar, the best place to stand the machine is in a room, into which the direct rays of the sun do not enter, and where there is little traffic. The machine, moreover, must stand perfectly steady, as. if subjected to shocks, there will be great likelihood of some of the chickens when hatched, being deformed.

The Temperature Of The Egg Drawer. A great variation in the temperature of the egg drawer has a bad effect upon the hatching results. Not only do fewer chickens make their appearance, but those that do make a successful exit are liable to be delicate. An incubator is automatically regulated, and thus once the apparatus is properly adjusted there is little difficulty in maintaining a regular temperature. Of course, at all limes something is liable to go wrong, and it is necessary to watch care- fully the regulator. In a tank machine the best temperature to work at is 104 degrees for hen and turkey eggs, and 103 degrees for duck and goose eggs. In the hot-air machine a tempera- ture of 103 degrees gives the best results for hen and turkey eggs, and 102 degrees for duck and goose eggs. Cooling And Turning.

The eggs within the incubator should be cooled twice a day, and while they are cooling they should be turned. A very common mistake is that the eggs are not cooled sufficiently long. The eggs must be cooled for a considerable period, otherwise the chickens will not be XS strong or as hardy when hatched. During the winter months, when the weather is cold, five minutes is quite long enough, whereas during the summer twenty-five and even thirty minutes is none too long. While the eggs are cooling they should be turned—not .end over end, but sideways. It simplifies matters to mark the eggs with an X on one side and an O. on the other, and it ensures that none are missed. Unless the eggs are regularly turned the partly- developed chicken is liable to stick to the shell. It is important to see that the hands are.quite clean while turning the eggs. Some Minor Points. •

Eggs during incubation must receive a sufficient supply of moisture. This is an essential factor towards good results. If there is a lack of moisture the inner and outer mem- branes—the two thin skins immediately within the shell—become thick and tough, and the chicken won't be able to pierce them. When there is a water tray provided this should always be kept filled with water. It is a good plan to dip the eggs into water, heated to about 100 degrees, on the nineteenth day of incuba- tion. This serves the useful purpose of soften- ing the inner and outer membranes, thus making the exit of the chicken more easy. Be sure that the lamp is kept clean and the wick trimmed properly. Upon the even working of the lamp a great deal ffepends.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
P 1111,1111ENT LOSSES.

Legislators Vlto Have Given Their , Lives For Country.

The year that closed yesterday saw an excep- tionally large number of deaths among mem- bers of the Legislature, mainly as a result of the active part taken in the war by politicians. In all 46 of those entitled to sit at Westminster twelve. months ago have passed away. Of these 31 were peers (in addition to five Irish peers who did not sit in the House of Lords), the remaining 15 being elected legislators. Six members of each Chamber have givf n their lives for the Empire during 1915. The peers who have been killed on active service or who have died of wounds were Lord Brabourne, LorcikKesteven, Lord Vernon, Lord de Freyne, Lord Petrie,. and the Earl of Seafield; while the M.P. soldiers were Mr. W. G. C. Gladstone, Mr. T. C. B. Agar-Robartes, Mr. H. T. Cawley, Mr. C. T. Mills, Lord Ninian Crichton-Stuart, and Mr. L. Walrond, -the latter dying as the result of illness contracted from exposure. While only five new creations have been made, eight names on the old roll of the House of Lords no longer appear through the titles lapsing, and an Irish earldom has also disap- peared for the same reason.

FARM AND GARDEN.

With regard to the agricultural industry, farwers have made great sacrifices and have put up with a shortage of 'labour which would have been very inconvenient but for the way in which women have, come 'forward and worked harder than they have ever done before—Mr. Runci- man, in the House of Commons.

Enormous quantities of acorns, it is said, are being imported into Germany from Holland at a cost of 6 'florins per hectolitre (21 bushels). They sell for 101 florins in Germany, where the tannic acid is first extracted and flour and bread are made from the residue.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN FOOD SHORTAGE.

In the suppressed " Vorwaerts " (the German Socialist paper) is a comparison of the cost of living before and during the mar. In August, 1914, the irreducible minimum for the average German household was 25.44 marks. In June, 1915, it was 37.36 marks. The increase in price, says the " Vorwaerts," has been accompanied with a decrease in quality, and this is stated as , follows, according to a translation in the " Out- I look ": "Meat less and poor; breadstuffs, in- cluding cheese, not to be enjoyed; about half the normal quantity of butter and eggs used; vegetables of the poorest sort in use; sugar much reduced; cocoa, tea and marmalade have almost disappeared from the table, even fresh fruit, so necessary for tle nourishment of chil- dren; potatoes and war bread have become the principal means of nourishment. The result is general under-nourishment. This means not only bad nourishment, but hunger, permanent hunger."

===== 0002601_19160101_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
PLOUGHING AND BURIED SHELLS.

An odd problem, which will be encountered all through the fighting territory after the war, is that of guarding against the exploding of shells which buried themselves in the ground without having exploded. In the section of France along the Marne, where the fighting was sharp early in the war, and from which the Germans re- treated, the peasant farmers already have found this danger to be real. A ploughman at any moment may strike a shell that will explode with sufficient force to kill him and his team. Various solutions have been suggested, but the most promising is one worked out by a French scientist and considered by the Academy of Science. The plan is to go over the farms with an electrical instrument that will give warning when a mass of metal is near. Such an instru- ment is an old American invention, frequently used in laboratory work. Some modifications of it were adopted by the French scientist to fit it for field use. In practical tests on the old battle- fields he found that it is possible for two men to explore an acre thoroughly in about one r hour, and locate every buried shell near I enough to the surface to do any harm.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
STEADY PRICES.

At a meeting of the Ilford Farmers' Assodia- tion, Mr. Edwin Savill gave an address on the subject of "The Agricultural Outlook as Affected by the War," and referring to prices, said:--

We now have high prices, in a few months we may have low prices, but what We want is steady prices. It is better for all of us to have some idea of what the result of our labours is to .be than to stand the chance of a high price which we never expected on the one hunt'', or to have to plough in our crop because there is no sale on the other. These things are not all due to outside influences; so far as they are you will have to see to it that you are protected against them. They are too often the result of inside influences; there are knock-outs in cattle markets as there are in furniture sales, the vegetable market is rigged as are stocks and shares, and the same applies to the milk market. Are you content that these things should continue? What is the agricultural out- look as affected by the war? How has the war affected the future of . agriculture? And I will answer that by ,asking another question. What effect has the war had upon you? for the prospects of agriculture depend upon you, and its future depends upon your efforts,. and I ask you, are you, the Ilford Farmers' Association, content to let things rip, or has the war had the effect of putting fresh energy into you? Cannot we all see things more clearly than we could two years ago; has not the war opened our eyes to possibilities? I think it has.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

TOP-DRESSING FRUIT TREES.

• This is the time of year when one finds the spade being plied vigorously among fruit trees. There is no doubt that this continual digging has much to answer ,for in the many , complaints one liears •as•to the non-cropping of , fruit trees. The most .vigOrOus- and healthy ' roots of well-managed fruit trtt.s are always to be found near the 'surface, and spreading out • to as far as the branches extend, but if this yearly mutilation of the roots is carried out little Wonder is it that these- fibrous roots are non-e*istet. This also, fainss the _roots to go down into the sub-soil, and causes no end of trouble in the way of canker and coarse, un- ripe wood. Far better use a steel fork, so that the roots may not be mutilated in any way, but the soil merely loosened among them. When this has been completed a top-dressing of loamy soil and rotten manure will feed the roots, and keep them at work near the surface. Rubbish, heaps, old hotbeds, and decayed leaves put through a coarse sieve come in well for the top- dressing, and if such material is put on yearly it is wonderful how quickly the roots of the trees are attracted to it.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
SELECTION OF FRUIT.

At this season,' when the planting of fruit trees is being done all over the country, many people are hesitating as to what varieties to select for their pal;ticular district. It is useless to say that only this or that variety should be planted, simply because there is no apple, or pear that will succeed alike in all soils and situations, as some varieties will grow and bear freely in one district and fail in others, there- fore the selection of varieties must be done by carefully noting those sorts that crop the best and most regularly in the immediate neighbour- hood. Another important point is whether easy, midseason, or late varieties will prove the most profitable.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
BURNT REFUSE.

The value of this in the garden during the spring season cannot be over estimated, and now is the time to prepare a stock of it. At this season all rubbish, including the prunings of fruit trees should be well charred, not burnt too much, but just sufficiently to crumble down. All rubbish which has been collected during the summer should be now well turned and intim- ately mixed, adding to it some lime to destroy any insects that may be present. In the course of turning pick Out all pieces of wood and throw them on the fire heap.—" Wick," in "Farm and Home." •

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JANUARY 1, 1911

THE OUTLOOK.

The outlook in, some respects upon, this opening day of another year is not quite what we should like to ,see it. We do not refer to the state of affairs in the field, or, to be accurate, in the fields, for we are involved in a surprising number of regions in arTned action. Whatever the Germans may say, or however many small peoples they may, in their delirium, trample upon, they are firmly held by their great opponents, the British, the French, the ivussians, and the Italians. In that grip they are bound to come to grief sooner or later; and some day we shall be free from the menace they have been to Euro- pean peace for a generation. No, we refer more to the state of affairs at home. Here

we appear to be entering upon a time of political crisis over the question of compulsion. As we have said in these columns more than once, compulsion was to be expected by those single men who failed to answer their coun- try's call, preferring to let married men fill the places they themselves should fill. But first of all, it must be proved that the single men have failed to do their duty.. The country has absolutely no data upon which to form an opinion. If the shirkers are a negligible quantity, we might, with advantage, leave them to stew in their own unpatriotic juice; if they are a number that would count, it might be worth making them do their duty. , But what we complain about is the attempt to stampede the Government into some form of conscrip- tion without the facts being produced. So far as we know, Lord Derby's scheme was a ntic success; his-Lordship said his workers

Its honour and freedom, its joy and mirth. To fling back the curse that would blight the earth— Sing of the boys who have gone!

Sing of the boys who,have gone From college and store and school, To the deafening roar of the shot and shell, The nerve-wracking strife that is worse than Hell, To be blinded or main:Lid—or worse—Who can tell?—

Sing of the boys who have gone:

Heroes all—the boys who have gone From mother and home and love! To win the world from its maddening craze Of despairing aims and barbarian ways, To a saner soul, and sunnier days— Sing of the boys who have gone!
