THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JANUARY 1, 1916.

OUT OF THE SHADOWS.

The Dawn Of A New Year And A Glorious Peace.

TRAGEDY TO BLESSING.

BY HAROLD MAYBURY.

The streets are deserted, for the hour is late, and soon 'twill be the dawn. Over all a silence, weird and full of phantasy, a time vithal when man's mind thinks of the passing of the years. There is no gleaming mantle of snow on tower or turret, on castle or cottage, mountain or glen, to remind one that this is winter, yet the old year is nigh gone. The earth seems at rest. Many heads are bowed in prayer, and in cathedral, village church, and humble cottage, a solenui hush is o'er all: The tragic year. of 1915 is nearly flown. In, sacred edifices men and women offer prayers and supplications unto their Maker; in humble homesteads throughout the land mothers and fathers of England's sons are silent with the thoughts of loved ones absent, and the moments pass on. Come, mothers of England, whose sons' blood has drenched the fair plains of Flanders, France, Serbia and Gallipoli, dry your tears! Fathers in old arm- chairs, why that look of utter despair? The tragic memories live only for a little while, but the glory of those fallen for honour, liberty, and justice remains for ever, until the sun has ceased to shine, or the stars in all the glory shall become cold, or moon devoid of brilliance and light. The sorrow and burden is overwhelming, the absence of your kith and kin, now gone to their last rest, seemingly too great to bear. But be ye comforted. In your hearts and minds to you come repeatedly the words, "There' is no God; there is no God."

===== 0002601_19160101_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
AS DAY FOLLOWS NIGHT.

Oh, ye of little faith! Look you through the windows at the starlit sky Hear the low moan of the troubled wind! Remember the coming of night and of the days through which you have passed, the dawn of still an- other year, the seasons, and of Nature, and maybe 'twill help you to understand! The old year is dying, soon shall be the ringing of the bells heralding the advent of another year, and in your loneliness the pro- clamation reminds you of their last messages. To you they seem like funereal notes, mock- ing and terrible, clanging into your very soul, reminders of your sorrow and of your loss. "Your son is dead, your son is dead!" seems to be borne in their echoes;• but it is not so. Listen! Do not the bells ring joyously and inspiringly, seeming to say, "Your son—he lives; your son—he lives " ? Yes, the human course is run, but the spiritual one is ever new and eternal.

On fields afar, stricken with the horrors of war, are rough mounds of earth,. barren and melancholy, tiny crosses bespeaking fallen heroes. At this hour the moon shines bright, casting its beams o'er the dotted plains, and that light is God's acknowledgment of duty performed. In narrow beds, with proud faces upturned towards their Maker, they lie at rest. The human form is still and cold, lifeless and grim, but the spirit has flown with the breeze. Mingling with the chimes of the bells and on angel's wings are being borne to you, in these the last moments of a tragic and grief-stricken year, your son's memories.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MASTER'S MESSAGE.

He is with you now, bidding you have courage, helping you in your sorrows and in your afflictions. He cannot speak, 'tis true, but nevertheless he can make one under- stand; stirring the soul in manner we poor mortals are not given knowledge to compre- hend. He who has gone before waits patiently, at peace, iii another world, where travail and the horrors of warfare, caused by man—yea, caused by man !—are unknown. "Lo, I am with you. always," was the Master's message, which, uttered in cen- turies past, stands out to-day, conspicuous and prominent, a beacon lighting up the world, now stricken and bleeding. In your anguish let that message be instilled into your mind, and it shall be comforting, words that shall help through the weary days and wakeful nights. The message is not only His words, but your son's; he is whispering it to you even now, calling it to you in your hour of trial, as `ho,looks into your eyes full of hope and under- standing.

• To him there- is 34,0 night, only an everlast- ing dawn, perfect and unclouded; no time, only eternity. And he is telling you that now, looking into your eyes steadfast, yet full of pity for that aching heart. He lingers at your side watching the tears flow copious and fast. • THE PROMISE OF THE FUTURE.

Hark ye to the bells! It is the dawn of a aew, year. , The old year is no more. Through the stillness of the night chants of praise are now being offered up to the All Highest for His goodness. Won't you, in solemn silence, do likewise, remembering all the while that he, your Son, is bY' your side? A new year, seeming to you a tragedy, a tale untold, and in your thoughts no hopes or prospects of gladness. 'What are the future days to you ? Listen! From out of the chaos of bloody war- fare there is even now a new and glorious dawn awakening, when men shall extend the hand.of sympathy to fellow man, suffering with those who suffer, joyous with those who are glad. In a little while there shall be peaCe again, a peace such as was never known before in the history of this our land. Your son, nay, Eng- land's son, will have been the means of attain- ing this glorious peace, and throughout the ages his and other names shall stand revered and sacred, not as a will o' the wisp, but as a niighty beacon shining in full glory to remind men of the higher purposes of mankind. As long as earth shall list the little mounds shall be lit up by Nature's blessings. At dawn, the faint rays of the sun, bespeaking a new day; at sunset, when the light is nigh gone, still shall they play on those hallowed spots, and ere it is dark, moon and stars will carry on the work of God's acknowledgment. A happy New Year! Come, fathers, mothers, sisters, brothers, help me echo it through the world! It, is God's New Year, and He in His goodness helps us to remember by the pealing of those chiming bells, only a few ,hours before the dawn, when o'er conti- nent and isle long shafts of light like spears aeross the sky-proelaim the birth of another day and of another year.

" Lo, I am with you always!" The dawn. It is His message. Will you not remember

"BANTAMS "'LEAVE WARRINGTON

The "Bantam "Battalion of the South Lanca- shire Regiment, which has been in training at the Orford Barracks since its formation, left Warrington on Thursday for Aldershot, where the men will undergo a further course of train- ing. The " Bantams " have reached a high state of efficiency. At Aldershot they will be joined by other "Bantams." They left the Barracks about 9.20, headed by a torchlight pr9cession, the band of the 2nd South Lancs. Regt. (in charge of Bandmaster Cahill) and the bugle band of the Crosfield Troop of Boy Scouts. The procession marchoi through the main streets to Bank Quay Station. the officers present including Captains Stott and Crane. Great enthusiasm was shown at the station, where a large crowd had collected. Thit Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) was present. As the train left the station about 11.15, the band played "Auld Lang Syne."

Sir John French Honours Twelve Local Officers and Men.

"GALLANT AND DISTINGUISHED SERVICES."

General Sir John French, in a communica-c tion to the Secretary of State for War, pub- lished to-day, says that in accordance with the last paragraph of, his dispatch, dated October, 15th, he has the honour to bring before the' notice of the War Office the names of those he recommends for gallant and distinguished services in the field.

Among the names thus honoured are the fol- lowing belonging to the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment : Lieut‘nant-Colonel B. Fairclough. Major G. R. Crosfield. Captain Egerton Fairciough. Second-Lieutenant F. J. Barnish. Second-Lieutenant .1. S. Frith. Second-Lieutenant S. T. Quint.

COLONEL B. FAIRCLOUGH.

Mentioned in Dispatches for Ilip second time. •

Lieutenant-Colonel Brereton Pairclough, who co.b ands our:Territorials, is the 'eldest son of r. Richard T. Fairelough, Barryfield, Latchfoid. He is 45 years ef age. In May, 1912, he • succeeded his cousin, Lieutenant-Colonel J. Dutton Pairclough, as commander of the Battalion.

. Colonel Fairolough served in the Boer War as a Volunteer.

Eight years ago he married Miss Ethel Roberts, daughter of Councillor Henry Roberts. He was mentioned in dispatches in June.

MAJOR GEOR6E CROSFIELD.

Mentioned in Dispatches for the

second lime.

Major George Rowlandson Crosfield, third son of the late Mr. John Crosfield, of Walton Lea, was born in 1877. At the outbreak of the war he was one of the directors of the firm of Messrs Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. and was responsible for the technical side Of the business.

Major Crosfield was in command of the Man- chester Squadron of Yeomanry in South Africa, was invalided home through enteric, and, on recovering, again went out as Officer Com- manding a squadron of the 3rd Contingent of Yeomanry. He is a bachelor. He was mentioned in dispatches in June.

QUARTERMASTER & H&\. LIEU T.

G. 11. INGRAM.

Mentioned in Dispatches.

Quartermaster Ingram has had a long and distinguished Army career, his total length of service being 32 years. He joined the Rifle Brigade at Aldershot in the year 1883, and since then he has seen service in many parts of the British Empire, including India, Egypt and South Africa. Just before the outbreak of the South African War he was transferred to the 3rd South Lancashire Regiment, joining he staff of the Ist Volunteers (as it was then) Battalion, and in 1902 he was appointed Instructor to the Mounted Infantry. He suc- ceeded Sergeant-Major Rawlinson in 1905 as Battalion-Sergeant-Major, and in that capacity he gave such a good, account of himself that he was promoted some "time afterwards to th rank of Quartermaster in the place of Major A. F. G. Potter, who- had resigned. The ap- pointment was extremely popular with both the ,officers and men of the 4th South Lanca- shire Regiment, and the Quartermaster.ha shown himself as capable in this rank, as ir his former ones.

The military correspondent of the "Times,' in an article on a visit to the Warrington Volunteer Camp at Llanberis, in August, 1907 thus alluded to Sergeant-Major Ingram "What, after all, is the cause of success in war but good work done in peace? The allot- ment of a small number of honours every year to Brigadiers and Commanding Officers who have worked up their commands to a high standard of efficiency would produce a good effect so long as honours are safely granted for efficiency and not for any other cause. There are also a number of admirable N.C.O.'s and Warrant Officers of the Permanent Staff, like Sergeant-Major Ingram, of the Ist V.B. South Lancashires, of whose services to the second. line it is difficult to speak too highly. We ought to look after these, people, since they are doing good work, ,and the best of the Voltillieers could not have reached their pre"- sent standard without them." - _

Quartermaster Ingram proceeded to Prance with. his Battalion .in the early part of..the year and has rendered such valuable services at the base that makes his reward very fitting.

SERGEANT\ HUGH CREAMER.

Mentioned in Dispatches.

Sergeant Hugh J. Creamer, who was wounded on June 22nd, resides, when at home, with his wife, at 'lBO, Wellfield-street. He received a slight wound in the arm during shelling, and 'his, ribs were also Tontused. Sergeant Creamer, has worked in Warrington for about ten years, and enjoys the respect and esteem of a large circle of acquaintances. A keen civilian soldier; -he-Was a member of the. old. Volunteers, and then joined in with the Territorials., .He .w.as among the ...first of his company to volunteer for active, service. Last' Christmas he was 'inade -a lanceeorporal While in the South of England. A month later he received his second stripe and became a full corporal, and shortly after landing in France he was awarded the coveted third stripe, and thus, was promoted to, his present rank. 'Sergeant Creamer' Works for Messrs. Cros; field, with whose employes he enjoyed great popularity and from .whom he ,has received many welcome little gifts during the time he has been fighting. at the Front. He was for- merly connected with the Crosfield Fire Bri- gade,: and he took an active interest in National 'Service League propaganda.,:render- ing valuable assistance to Major GeOrge Cros- field in this connection. He is a member of the Crosfield Operatic Society, and also of the Borough Band, being an enthusiastic and competent instrumentalist.' He is 31 years of age.

SERGEANT TONI M IDD.

Mentioned in Dispatches.

h...----

Corporal James Albert (Bert) Vale, 96, Cartwright-street, was in the great push in ISeptember, when a section of 'our local Territorials—the bomb-throwers—who were attached to the Gordon Highlanders, were in the thick of the fray, storming a German redoubt. It was in this heroic struggle that the daring work of Corporal Vale came under the notice of an officer of a Scottish regiment, who sub- 'sequently informed Vale that he would re- commend his conduct to higher quarters. In that engagement Vale received a slight wound which necessitated his retirement from the trenches for a short time. He soon recovered, but on October 28th he was accidentally wounded. The bomb-throwers were having a little practice when one of the men slipped whilst in the act of throwing a bomb. The bomb only travelled about a dozen yards, when it exploded close to where Corporal Vale was standing, and he received two wounds in the leg. He was conveyed to England and was a patient at the Wharncliffe Hospital until a fortnight ago, when he came to Warrington on sick furlough. , Corporal Vale ig 32 years of age and resides with his wife and four children at 96, Cart- wright-street. In civilian life he was em- ployed in the stores department of the London and North-Western Railway Locomotive De- partment. He is an old Volunteer and joined "D" Company, later transferring to the Mounted Infantry. When the Territorial Forces were founded he was one of the first to join the A.S.C. attached to the Warrington TerritorialS. :On' the 'oUtbrea.k• of.'war he re- enlisted in the Territorials, and was in the Battalion which sailed for France in February of last year. He was promoted to lance. corporal later, and after the attack in Septem- ber he was given a further stripe to rank as corporal.

PRIVATE JOSEPH CLARKE.

Mentioned in Dispatches.

Private Joseph Clarke, whose home is at 134, Gorsey-lane, where his wife and one young child are residing; is only 24 years of age. He has worked ort the oil plant at Crositadas for fire or

six years, and his father, Mr. John Clarke, 59, Slater-street, Latchford, also works for the same firm in the caustic department. When the war broke out Private Clarke had served his four years in the Territorials, but he immediately re-joined. In the kitchen of his neatly-kept home hangs a framed intimation to the follow- ing effect:

" Your commanding officer and Brigade Com- mander have informed me that you distin- guished yourself by your conduct in the field on June 16th, 1915. I have read their report with much pleasure, and have brought it to the notice of higher authority.

" Major-General." Clarke is a stretcher-bearer, and he received the 'following letter from Mrs. J. J. Crosfield, Netherdale, Frodsham, whose son Eric was wounded in the great charge :--- " Dear Mr. Clarke,—Major Crosfield, whom we saw in London the other day, when he was on leave, told us or your goodness in searching for our son and the long time you devoted to the search after the tattle on the 16th, and so I. write you to express the heartfelt thanks of m- husband and myself for your kind action. It was indeed good of you, for you must have been very worn and weary yourself at the time."

PROMOTED.

Commission For Mr. Frank Stansfield.

Sergeant Francis 0. Stansfield, son of Mr. W. H. Stansfield, Salisbury-street, head master of St. James' School, Latchford, has been recom- mended for a commission while on active service in France. Sergeant Stansfielll, who was articled to Mr. Longland, solicitor, originally attended the Liverpool College and was a member of the Officers' Training Corps connected with that institution..t In the second month of the war he joined thi3•iting's Liverpool Regiment, and was promoted lance-corporal, corporal and then sergeant. A Master of the Liverpool College

recently described Mr. Stansfield as follows: " Stanstieild—a young giant, with a sergeant's stripes and a sergeant's vigour and alertness— was also a welcome visitor." _

Early in life Mr. Frank Stansfield was in- terested in military matters and invented a war game that was highly spoken of by military experts.

An intimation that his son was to receive a commission was received this week by Mr. W. H. Stansfield, from the Front.

, After speaking of the strenuous time he and the men have had during Christmas, and say- ing that he is writing within 700 yards of the Germans, he quite casually mentions that yesterday morning ' he was sent for by the Colonel, who told him he was recommended for a commission, and said some complimentary things concerning him. Sergeant Stansfield, who was as surprised as he was delighted, does not know the regiment to which he will be sent.

Those who nave watched the career of Mr. Frank Stansfield feel that the honour he has gained has been well won, and that he will fill any position to which he may be called with dignity and success.

CONCERT AT THE FRONT. Humour At Christmas In The

Trenches.

Corporal Leonard Taylor, son of Mr. D. ,G. Taylor, of Walter-street, Padgate-lane, who spent Christmas in the trenches, has come home this week for a short holiday. Being in the Army Reserve, he was called up on the out- break of war, and has seen service at Monk, on the Marne and on the Aisne, and has, naturally, some very, interesting experiences stored up for the time when it will be oppor- tune to tell them. In civil life he is in the telephone department of the Wigan Post Office, and is married. He has had one brother killed —Trooper Sidney George Taylor, of the 3rd Dragoon Guards—as previously recorded in "The Examiner," and another brother—Sapper Guy Taylor—is, like himself, in the Royal Engineers. It will be seen from the programme reproduced below of the concert that was given in the neighbourhood-of the trenches at Christ- mas, and at which Corporal Taylor was pre- sent, what is the spirit animating our men at the Front after a year and a half of strenuous campaigning:—

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE.

Births.

Last week there were 30 birth... in Warring- ton (14 male and 16 female) against 21 in the corresponding week last year.

Infectious Disease.

The following fresh cases a infectious disease were reported last week in Warrington:— :Scarlet fever, 6; diphtheria, 1..; tuberculous phthisis, 3; erysipelas, 1. There are 77 scarlet lever cases in the hospital.

Acknowledgment. Mrs. W. T. Derry, of the Bewsey Manse, acknowledges with thanks, a parcel received from the Needlework Guild.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
Last Week's Deaths.

Last week there were twelve deaths in War- nington from the following causes:—Scarlet -fever,l; diphtheria, 1; phthisis, 2; bronchitis, 3; pneumonia, 1; diseases and occidents of par- -turition, 1; all other causes, 3. In the eorre- fiponding week last year there wee 21 deaths.

At St. Peter's.

Christmas carols were rendered by St. Peter's choir at the evening service on Sunday. and were thoroughly enjoyed. Solos were perfectly rendered by Miss Amy Holmes, Mr. H. Sudlow, Mr. T. Walker, and Master J. Padmore. Mr. E. A. Padmore presided at the organ. As Sunday was St. Stephen's Day, the Rev. J. Martin (curate) gave a short address on Saint Stephen.

School Children Entertain the Wounded.

The scholars of the Sacred Heart School entertained the wounded soldiers at the White- cross Hospital on the Thursday before Christ- -147ms. The programme included a number of songs and dances. At the conelu,ion the chil- dren were thanked by Mr. Loxham and pro- vided with refreshments. Father Rockliffe was in charge of the young entertainer,.

Billiards.

• The winners at the Latchford Conservative Club billiard handicap for prizes---given by, Messrs. C. Garton Stone, D. Watson, J. Coxon, A. Ashton, P. Short, A. Chester, and Councillor Wilson—were: J. Ashurst, P. Harbourne, W. Bitter, S. Bradshaw, A. Cooke. J. Orford, A. 'Grayson, J. Thompson, J. Hanson, H. Drum- mond, C. Davenport, R. florton, G. Newall, T. Connor, G. R. Parker, W. Counor. jun., J. Gibbs, A. Gorton, E. Leigh, A. Daintith and F. Fennelly. • Collections for the Belgians.

At every house in the borough -7and the, sur- Tounding district on Christmas Day, a collec- tion was taken at the dinner table for the pur- pose of providing food for the Belgians who Are under German rule in Belgium, and it is pleasing to note that up to Thursday evening 2161-Is. 11d, had been sent in by the collectors. This sum includes £25 from Mr. C. E. Parker and. £1 from the Bewsey Adult School-. Various' organisations have not yet sent in their collee- Along, and when these arrive, no doubt the sum already obtained will be considerably increased.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Children's New Year Parlv.

_ We hear that the tickets for the Children's New Year party at the John Crosfield Memorial Hall are selling splendidly. They are is. each, and the whole proceeds from their 'sale will go to augment the funds of the war sewing class of the Women's Liberal Association. The class :received a very acceptable and welcome Christ- mas gift of £25 from Lady Crosfield. Lady Crosfield and the ladies connected with the sewing class are providing presents, one of which will be given to each of their youthful guests.

On Thursday week there will be a whist drive, at which the Mayor and Mayoress will be present, in aid of the same funds.

Wood's College Removes in Bridge-

street.

It will be seen from an advertisement on our front page that Wood's College, which has had such a successful run in Bold-street. is to be transferred to more central and convenient premises next week. When the new term starts on Monday, pupils will assemble in the front rooms over the new City and Midland Bank premises, said to be the most up-to-date class- rooms in this neighbourhood.

Mr.' Wood has been. highly successful in training young people for business careers, and very many of his pupils are now occupying positions with firms in this locality. He has been in Warrington now for a considerable number of years and has gained for himself a regatation for turning out expert shorthand writers and typists. Students are trained for business, civil service and bank appointments at day and evening classes and by private lessons.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Carols at the Parish Church.

The choir at the Parish Church rendered a number of Christmas carols at the evening service on Sunday. The carols included a num- ber of old-time favourites and one or two not so familiar, but they were all admirably sung, the choir showing an excellent knowledge of the expression as well as the technique of the pieces. The selections included "Bethlehem," "On • Christmas Morn," "The Manger 'Throne," "The Carol of the Star," "Across the Snow," "Shepherds, shake off your drowsy sleep," "In Sorrow and in Want." and "Good Xing Wenceslas." The soloists in this latter splendidly rendered their parts. As treble and baritone, respectively, Master Neville. Davies .and Mr. Walter Dennett interpreted the music with good effect. Mr. P. M. Darby was at the organ and performed his share of the service in his usual praiseworthy manner. The Rector, 'towards the close of the service, wished the mcmgregation every blessing of the season.

• - New School in Vt'arringlon. During the last three years a chain of about :forty Commercial Schools, all united,wider one :governing body, has been established in Great Britain. These schools are known; (la tbe De !Bear Schools, and specialise in modern. business 'training. They are under the direct control of Mr. Bernard de Bear, probably the most pro- minent figure in the British Isles in commer- c4al education. Mr. de Bear, prior. to taking up this work, was; for 25 years, head of the Pitman's School in London.

Since the war started these schools have ,grown enormously, and they are said to be ,t-loing great work in preparing our young -people both to meet the great demand of the -present in business and to take part in that -rejuvenation of the commercial greatness of the 'British Isles which is bound to come after this ‘war.

There are several features of interest in this new organisation. In the first place it has treat :facilities for placing its students in suitable positions anywhere in the British,lsles, and is. at the present moment, in the predicament of •being unable to turn out students anything like fast enough for the demands that are made ..upon -rt. On Monday .one of these schools will he opened at Buttermarket-street, corner of Bank. street, Warrington, under the charge of .Mr. .Norman W. Wood. Complete courses of -struction for business careers have been ar- ranged, and fine premises and equipment in- stalled. We feel sure that this new school will '-he an acquisition to the educational facilities .of our town.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
The Conservative Club.

Sir Gilbert Greenall presided it the annual' -meeting of the members of the Warrington Con- •tiervative Club, held last week. The annual report, presented by Mr. Henry Collin (hon. secretary) stated that in the eir- -cumstances the membership was satisfactory. 'The bowling mains, billiard handicaps, and -other entertainments for prizes had not been held this year.. No billiard league matches had 'taken place, and the club were the holders of the "Harold Smith" Cup and the "Crosfield " 'Challenge Shield the same as last year. The ,committee had loaned to the Soldiers' Home in Bold-street a billiard table .for the duration of the war, and it had proved a great boon, and was much appreciated. There were about fifty members on active service, and they bad been made honorary members of the club for the -period of the war.

Mr. W. H. Bellian, the hon, treasurer, who has obtained a commission in the Army, was unable to he present, and Mr. R. H. Dain.tith, who has been appointed to act as his deputy, reported that the financial position of the club continued to be good. Sir Gilbert Greenall, on behalf of the mem- bers, presented a handsome timepiece (supplied by Messrs. Eustance and Co.), to Mr. and Mrs. James Holland, the late steward and stewardess, in recognition of their services to the club. Mr. and Mrs. Holland are succeeded by Mr. OW' Mrs. Pritchard, of the Oxton Conservative anb.

A new ambulance class for men will begin on Friday next. The cost per head will be 3s. 6d., and candidates are being enrolled by Supt. Baldry at the Police Station.

As a result of a collection taken at the per- formance of Handel's "Messiah," by the War- rington Musical Society in the Parish Church on Thursday evening of last week, the handsome sum of £1.4 is. 3d. was realised. This will be handed over to the local War Funds.

The "Tabernacle."

A series of special addresses on "The Taber- nacle," will be given at the Gospel Hall, in Forster-street, next week, commencing to- morrow night. The speaker will be Mr. it. Wilson, of Kendal, and his remarks' will be illustrated by diagrams.

"The Men England Needs." The Rev. B. Fell will deliver a lecture at the Primitive Methodist Church, Legh-street, on Thursday evening. His subject will be "The Men England Needs." In the afternoon he will preach a sermon.

To Enrol Recruits.

An officer of the Army Service Corps, Mechanical Transport Branch. will attend at the Recruiting Office, Police Buildings, on Thursday next to enlist men of the following trades: Motor mechanics, turners and copper- smiths.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Latchford Cadet Corps.

A very enjoyable hot-pot supper -was held by the members• of the Latchford Cadet Corps, in the Cairo-street Schools, on Thursday evening. The room was tastefully decorated with bunting and festoons, which added to the bright and cheerful atmosphere, which prevailed. His Worship the Mayor (Alderman Peacock) was present. The health of the King was proposed by Mr. J. B. Starkey, who said that he was sure all wished his Majesty a speedy recovery, and soon hoped to hear of him taking his place in public affairs again, and it was earnestly hoped he would soon be at the head of a nation at peace with the whole world—a peace with honour and freedom. In introducing the Mayor, Mr. Starkey said all had looked forward to his WorShip's presence, and all joined in wishing him a very happy and successful year. His kindness in coming to spend the evening with them was much appreciated. In addressing the Cadets, the Mayor said he was very pleased to come amongst them. His start in life was no better than any, of the boys there, if as good. He had not been born with a silver spoon in his mouth, he had worked hard, and had taken advantage of all oppor- tunities that had come his way. "You have not joined the Corps for fun, though you cer- tainly have a little fun now and again, but you are now fitting yourself for a higher position— if not for a mayoral position. I did not get to my present position in. Warrington through being pushed there. The discipline and train- ing you are now receiving will make 'you better citizens. For the past two years I have devoted my time to the interests of the town, and since the war began I have spent an anxious time. have had much responsibility, and very many duties to perform. " Although I had poor parents I have managed to obtain a good position, and you should strive to get as good, or a better one. I take a great interest in Cadet Corps, and I take this opportunity to thank Mr. Starkey for the valuable assistance he has given me on many occasions at processions and other public affairs. I feel very grateful for the ser- vices rendered. I wish the Corps every success,' and that its numbers might increase." During the evening the Quartermaster, G. B. Starkey. contributed several humorous selections. The accompanist was Miss G. Starkey. In con- clusion. Mr. Starkey suitably thanked the Mayor for his kindness, in which the Corps joined heartily. The Mayor has promised to become an honorary member of the Corps.

LABOUR AND DRINK.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Recently the newspapers published the testimony of Mr. J. S. Dane, fresh from Russia, as to the ability of the Russian dock labourers to endure fatigue and to do heavy work with- out the aid of intoxicants. Subsequently they were also kind enough to insert some evidence which I sent them as to the large proportion of English teetotal workmen who take their share of hot and exhausting toil at forge and furnace. With your permission I should like to give your readers a fresh and interesting piece of evidence as to the approval which the abolition of alcohol secures, alike from employ- ers and employed, after a fair trial of the new conditions. The evidence is contained in a letter which The MacLeod of MacLeod received from an English gentleman of his acquaint- ance who is a large employer of labour in Russia. With The MacLeod's permission I give the following extract : " I shall not be surprised to hear that Parlia- ment is debating the great question of obli- gatory • military ' service and the 'abolition of strong drinks. Heroic measures are called for during such times, and is it not wonderful that Russia should in this respect have shown the :example? Our workmen had a meeting the other day, which ended with a .unanimous vote for the total abolition of alcoholic drinks for ever. If England cannot give up races and strong drinks, &c., at such a time, she deserves the designation the Germans have given her of a decadent nation."

• • ALEXANDER THOMSON. Parliamentary Agent, United Kingdom Alliance. 11, Tothill-street, Westminster, December 29th, 1915.

ZILLAH BOAT WRECKED IN STORM.

Warrington Captain Saved.

The relatives of Thomas Ford, captain of the s.s. Cecil, owned by the Zillah Shipping Com- pany, which was wrecked off St. Quentin's Point, France, received information yesterday that their son was safe.. Mr. Thomas Ford resides With his ,parents at 42, Hood-lane, Sankey. For a number of years he has been employed by the Zillah Shipping Company. A few months ago the s.s. Cecil was engaged as a transport. In a letter dated December 27th, from Le .Crotoy, France, reporting the disaster, Mr. Ford states that the Cecil was trying to save s.s. Lady Iveagh, of Dublin, from going on a bank near St. Quentin's Point in a storm, when she was wrecked. All the crew, with the exception of the chief engineer, were quite safe.

GENERAL BOOTH.

Confidence And Hope Despite Riser,

Of Tears.

General Booth, in his New Year Message te the Salvation Army, says The Old Year is passing away, as I write, amidst rivers Of blood and tears. Nevertheless, I. call on you to greet 1916 with confidence and hope. The past is gone; the future is ours. • Yesterday is like water that is gpilt upon the ground which can- not be gathered up 'again— Only its record remains.

"That cannot be altered. When'the die is cast, it is cast. No, nothing can now be changed into yesterday. But to-day—to-day is the hard fact of life which can be transmuted, by God's blessing, into riches that will never perish—into service that brings unending re- wards—into love that will last for ever. That is what we have got to do with the New rear."

CHRISTMAS WITH "TOMMY."

The Festive Season In Our Hospitals And Institutions.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
JOY AMIDST SUFFERING.

One of the chief traits in the many-sided character of the British " Tommy " is cheer- fulness. Whether at home or abroad, in the trenches or in hospital, he wears a smile that is all-conquering, and makes him a happy hero. Whilst during the past Christmas the civilian community has borne itself with quietness, the hospitals which contain our soldiers have been full of mirth and frivolity..

Generally speaking, Christmas in Warrington has been quiet, but on Christmas Eve the town was crowded. Services were held at most of the churches on Christmas Day, and at the Anglican

and Catholic places of worship large numbers of people made their Christmas Communion.

from their expressions, were very pleased with the catering and Erplendid ornamentations.

The Colonel's Good Wishes.

Whilst there, Colonel Moggridge, in a brief speech, first wished all a merry Christmas, after- wards expressing the hope that ere another Christmas Day had dawned the present mighty efforts put forward by England and the Allies would have brought about lasting results, and that victory would crown their glorious work. Lieut.-Colonel Ritchie heartily endorsed Col- Moggridgeis good wishes, at the same time re- minding the soldiers that the excellent arrange- ments were due to the kindly thoughtfulness and consideration of Regimental Sergt.-Major Quick, who, during the past' few days, had worked exceedingly hard so that those present should have a right royal time., Toasts were raised to the King, Colonel Meg- gridge, Lieut.-Colonel Ritchie, and others, the last, but not least, being one in memory of the boys in the trenches. A Splencejd Concert.

After dinner the troops were relieved and did as inclination suggested. Tea passed off smoothly, after which a concert was held, Col.

The matrons of the various wards, with their staffs and the men had decorated all the wards in a manner which would.have reflected credi. on professional decorators. On Christmas afternoon a concert was held in the large hall of the hospital, the more seriously indisposed being conveyed to the hall in wheeled chairs With one exception the whole of the artistes were from the hospital itself. The exception was Mr. J. H. Draper, the well-known Warrington singer, who very pleasingly rendered the songs "My sweetheart When a Boy" and "The Stars of Normandy." It is not often that Mr. Draper's Voice is put to so severe a test as on this occasion, but he was distinctly heard throughout the great building— the-ball being more capacious than any other in,, this part of the country—and his singing was much appreciated by the whole of the patients. Jones, "Bedouin's Love Song"; Sister Hunter, "Land of the Almond Blossoll " and "Jest Her Way "; Private R Pollington, a sword dance; Sergeant Cole, mandoline solo, "Sunshine of Her Smile "; Private C. Gordon, "Mate o' Mine." 'Corporal Sutherland, Private Huggins and • Private Pollington contributed a rag dance. The accom- panists .were Sergeant Marsh, Sergeant Smalley, Private Clair and Mr. Bow.

A cinematograph entertainment was provided in the Recreation Hall, where two shows are given weekly by the kindness of Mr. Chas Ashton. of the Grand. The large hall had beer nicely decorated for the concert, and contained two large Christmas trees, adorned with flags of the Allies and electric lights. Boxing Day Gifts.

On Boxing Day each wounded soldier received a gift which had been partly provided out of the money raised at the recent " 'Pommies' " Tea held at the Parr Hall. A singing and whistling competition was held on Monday, and this displayed the large amount of talent amongst the soldiers.

The decorations were kept pp until Tuesday, when hundreds of people again visited the hos- pital. They were all deeply impressed by the arrangements that had been made tor the enjoyment of the Christmas season and the smile that all the men were wearing proved that they had thoroughly enjoyed themselves.

THE WORKHOUSE.

As in other years, no difference was made in respect to Christmas arrangements at the Workhouse and the Workhouse Hospital, yet the inmates, in spite of the present crisis, were as buoyant and welcomed the festive season in a manner most delightful to witness. The dinner served was an excellent one, and con- sisted of roast beef, vegetables, plum pudding and dessert, which was thoroughly enjoyed, as was the entertainment in the evening, which had been arranged with especial care by Mr. W. W. Whittle. The wards of the hospital, in 'keeping with the season, were decorated in praiseworthy manner by the sisters, flags, ever- greens and flowers adding pleasing colour to the scene. Amongst those who paid a 'visit during the day were the Mayor and his son, Master Reginald Peacock, the Rev. E. Downham (acting chaplain), the Rev. H. Ogden (Non- conformist chaplain), Messrs. W. Furness (vice-chairman of the Board of Guardians), George Mather, W. C. Brooks, 3fr. A. ribttem- ley (clerk), Mr. R. I. Partridge (chief assistant clerk), Dr. J. R. The Mayoress was unable to be present owing to a slight illness.

CHRISTMAS AT THE INFIRMARY.

Wednesday was the big day at the Infirmary. but the festivities this year have not been on as great a &ale as formerly. Christmas Day was spent very quietly. The dinner in the male surgical ward was presided over by Mr. James Henry Smethurst (the chair- man of the Board of Management) and he was assisted in the work of serving the dinner by Mr. Roger C. Parr (hon. treasurer of the Institu- tion). The visitors during the day included Drs. Fox, Segal. and Binns, and Mr. Henry Collin. The male patients were supplied with tobacco, pipes, cigarettes and chocelate, and the women and children, with chocolate, fruit and crackers. The arrangements, which were successfully carried out, were in the hands of Miss Hammond (the matron). In Lieu of the Christmas Tree.

On Wednesday, instead of the usual Christ- mas tree being provided, gifts were distributed in the women's ward by the Mayor (Alderman Peter' Peacock). Amongst those who were present:' at the gathering were :—Doctors Bowden, Binus, Hutton, Segar and Murray, the Rev. H. V. Pigot; and Messrs. J. Fairclough, F. A. Frost, W. A. Proud, W. Dainteth and H. Collin. • After Mr. E. Wilkes' orchestra had played a. few selections, _

Mr. J. H. Smethurst said that as the Insti. tution was increasing year by year it was not possible for them to do as they wished in the way of entertaining visitors. Their only thought was that the patients and staff should have as good a time as possible. They had not got a Christmas tree, but though there was a great war raging, it was felt that no one in the Infirmary should he deprived of some reminder of Christmas, even in the midst of the trouble through which they were passing. He had re- ceived letters of apology from Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, Mr. Roger Parr, Mrs. Charlton Parr, Mrs. Armitage (Newton Bank), and also from Mr. and Mrs. W. Peter Rylands, Mrs. Geddes, and Mr. George Dakin and his 'wife. Unfortunately it had not been possible, owing to the heavy calls that had been made on the larger places of amusement, to make a system- atic collection this year, but the proprietors of two of the four picture palaces had been good enough to put a slide on the screen appealing for funds. (Applause.) Mayor Distributes Gifts.

The Mayor then proceeded to distribute the gifts to the patients and staff. In all 120 were distributed, and at the conclusion Mr. Sme- thurst mentioned that Sister Baxter was leav- ing the Infirmary after being there for five years, to take up a position as Matron in a Derbyshire Hospital. Dr. Bowden proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor, and this Was seconded by Mr. John Fairclough.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
A Valuable Institution.

Replying, the Mayor said that his duties that afternoon had been very light. He was only sorry that the Mayoress could not be present owing to her being ill. He was pleased to see how happy the patients were with their gifts, which had been subscribed for by the general public. They had to thank Mr. Smethurst for his untiring efforts to secure the money to make Christmas a little pleasant and to remind those at the Infirmary of Christmas time. He felt sure that Warrington was proud of the Infir- mary, and when the people knew that there was a deficiency they would make good the amount to enable the good work to go on. (Applause.) The ward had been well decorated with; flags, 4c., and presented, a festive appearance. A number of visitors were present and most of these spent part of the evening with the patients.

RADDON COURT HOSPITAL.

Thanks to the many energetic workers at the Raddon Court Hospital, Christmas Day was spent in a most enjoyable fashion, and will be remembered by the inmates for many years to come. On Christmas morn nearly all the patients attended Divine Service in chapels or churches in the immediate vicinity. Prior to the servingi of a seasonable dinner, which consisted of tuileys, beef, vegetables and plum puddings, the Commandant (Miss S. Broad- bent) read the Christmas greeting received from Their Majesties the King and Queen, which evinced hearty applause. Some amusement was caused during the eating of the plum pudding, for the soldiers had been informed of the fact that trinkets and silver coins had been placed inside during the making. As announced in our last week's issue, the wards were prettily ' and effectively decorated, the work and study of the patients being highly commendable.. Chinese lanterns, flags, festoons and paper rosettes were innumerable, which added greatly to the gaiety of the proceedings. Immediately on entering the hospital was seen a gigantic Christmas tree, lighted by elec- tricity and loaded with gifts for the soldiers. The presents were distributed by Mrs. J. Charlton Parr, who spoke cheering words to the patients, especially to those who could not, owing to injuries, leave their beds. Tea followed, which t was excellent in every respect, after which games and music followed, which were kept up with gusto until eight o'clock.

On Boxing Day the patients journeyed to the Royal Court Theatre to witness the performance of "Betty," they being conveyed there and back in a special tramcar, provided by the manager of the Corporation Tramways. The noise of the soldiers blowing trumpets and playing musical instruments afforded much amusement to pedestrians.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
CHRISTMAS TRAFFIC AT THE POST OFFICE.

The postal authorities at Warrington, in common with many other towns, experienced for the first time for many years a striking decrease in the amount of traffic at Christmas time.

There was a marked falling off in the posting and delivery of letters, &c. The posting of parcels was spread over a longer period than usual owing to the limit imposed on the date of posting for the Expeditionary Forces. The number of parcels for delivery compared very favourably with other years, there being only a very slight decrease. It was found necessary to employ women for sorting and delivery duties, and it must be said, in spite of the arduous nature of the work, it was performed in a most satisfactory manner. There was a marked increase of surcharged correspondence, and it is evident that the public have not yet accustomed themselves to the new rates of postage which recently came into operation.

The packing of parcels by the public has received more attention this year and, as a consequence, very few articles were.fOund loose or without addresses.

wounded on the sth December. The news first reached Mr. Beswick through the mediom ii the following letter, dated December 6th, itral signed F. G. Goddard, army chaplain:— "I am the chaplain here—with the I 2nd Field Ambulance—and write this letter fiir your boy Ted. Don't worry when I tell you that he came in here last night with a piece of Nhrap- nel through his left arm. He was brJaAht in shortly after being hit, which was about 5 p.m. He is all right in himself, and is very plucky and cheerful. We gladly do our best to look after our chaps. This is the dressing station, and Ted will be moved to-day down to the clearing station, then to the Base, and possibly to England. He is just going off, and will doubtless be able to send you further news. Don't fret or worry, but remember him in your prayers."

===== 0002601_19160101_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
The Germans' Busy Day.

Writing later himself, Private Beswick said: "I was hit on Sunday, the sth of December, which, as all the lads know, is the Germans' busy day. They must save all the shells they can during the week in order to give us a warm reception on the Sunday. I hope you did not upset yourself, for I am in no danger, but my arm is very sore. We had been working on a communication trench near to the firing line, and the enemy had been throwing 'iron foundries' up and down all day. I 'sailed clear,' as usual, and when we had finished we went back to the dug-out, only to find my mate building it up. Of course we had to give him a lift, when all at once we heard a shell coming. Of course we all threw ourselves down, and a piece struck me in the left arm, and my braces also were broken." Private Beswick is now in hospital in Ireland. He is 21 years of age, and was educated at the St. Thomas' School, Stockton Heath. He enlisted in February, having previously been employed by the Manchester Ship Canal Co., on the river diversion at Walton. He formerly be- longed to the St. John's, Higher Walton, Foot- ball Club, and also took an interest in bowls. Another brother, William, is in training with the 2/4th Battalion of the Territorials in the South.

One Of Four Soldier-Brothers Wounded In The Arm. One of the lour soldier sons of Mr. John Cooper, of 159, Cumberland-street, Latchford,

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

WOUNDED.

LANCE-CORPL. J. GREENWOOD.

Miraculous Escape From Death By Ills

Own Bomb.

Mrs. J. Greenwood, 8, Glazebrook-street, Man- chester-road, a few days ago received the news that her husband, Lance-Corporal Joseph Green- wood,. was suffering from bomb wounds on the hand.

The lance-corporal, who is in the King's Liverpool Regt., had been throwing bombs into the German trenches for two hours, and was expected to be engaged on this particular class of work for some days. However, he was throwing a bomb when it accidentally hit the top of the trench he was standing in, and it went off instantly, with the result that the top of his left thumb was blown off, pietes of shell lodged in the palm of his hands and his first finger was burst. Writing to his wife he states that it was a miracle he was not blown to pieces. He is at present an inmate of the North Evington War Hospital, Leicester, and is pro- gressing favourably. His wife and mother visited him on Sunday last and found him in the hest of spirits, in spite of his recent opera- tion. Lance-Corporal Greenwood is an old sol- dier, for he was in the National Reserve at the outbreak of war. He left for the Front in November, 1914, and returned in the following January, suffering from pneumonia. He went back again to the firing line in May, and has

LANCE-CORPORAL J. GREENWOOD. Wounded.

been through many stirring engagements, in- cluding the great advance of September 25th. Engaged at Ryland: Bros.

Before the war he was employed in the patent house at Messrs. Rylands's works, and wor- shipped at the Parish Church. Lance-Corporal Greenwood has sent home many souvenirs of the war, which .include a respirator, 'bullets, a ring and several sets of false teeth picked up in the German trenches.

DIED OF WOUNDS.

Official News Concerning A

Former Footballer.

Rifleman James Berry, of the Rifle Brigade, whose death in action was unofficially reported in September, is now officially stated to have died of wounds. The news from the War Ofre has been received by his wife, who resides at Albert Terrace, Howley. In addition, the following letter, dated December 15th, has come to hand from Major W. G. M. Sorel:— " I am in receipt' of your letter of the Ist. and regret that you have not as yet heard from the War Office. We have forwarded your letter through the proper channels, and no doubt yor will hear from the War Office shortly. The following are the details of your husband's death: Died of wounds, 8/9/15; buried in ceme- tery, by Red House, at . The cliapltiin attached to the battalion has. I believe, already written to you on the subject. Your hnsband's name has appeared in the casivilzy ti-ts pub- lished in the English papers. Please accept my deepest sympathy in your loss." The letter from the chaplain which referred to in the above letter,, has not, as yet, been received by Mrs. Berry. Private Berry, who was formerly an old War- rington Rugby player. was 47 years of age. He was formerly employed by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co. as a painter, and left there in September, 1914, to enlist. He was a keen angler and bowler, and belonged .to the Ancient Order of Shepherds. He was educated at Heathside School.

DAY OF NATIONAL PRAYER.

Mayor To .Attend The Parish Church.

In response to the suggestion of the Lord Mayor of London to the municipal authorities of the kingdom that there should be an official attendance of the Corporations at church on Suhday, the day of national prayer, in each city or borough, so far 188 of the Mayors and Provosts have sent cordial affirmative replies, stating that such attendance had :teen arranged. The intercession services at the Warrington Parish Church commenced on Friday evening. Processions were formed at the Church Insti- tute, St. George's Mission and St. Clement's Mis- sion and these proceeded to the Parish Church. To-day (Saturday) is to be kept as a day of continual prayer, and to-night there will be 'a meeting of communion and a meeting of inter- cession. On Sunday, in addition to the usual services being held, the Mayor and members of the Corporation will attend an intercession ser- vice at 3 p.m.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
In The Free Churches.

A form of service for use in the Free Churches for to-morrow's Day of Intercession, has been prepared by the National Council of Evangelical Free Churches, which may be used in its entirety or form the basis of the services in Nonconformist Churches.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's Presbyterian Church.

The Presbyterian Church of England has also decided to observe the first Sunday in the year as a day of national intercession. At St. John's Church the services of the day will be- come acts of special intercession. In the even- ing the Mayor will attend in his official capacity and take ,part in the service. The special evening offering will be devoted to the needs of the Joint Committee of the British Red Cross Society and the Weer of St. John of Jerusalem, which, according to Lord Lans- downe, reaches an average of something over £25,000 per week.

'EA LER

and card, together with the parcel which I re- ceived last night. The contents are. grand and will help to add a little cheer during the com- ing days. It is Christmas Ere as I write and we are in the trenches .for a few hours. "We shall come out early Christmas morn- ing, returning on Christmas night, and I ex- pect after sampling the many good -things sent out to us we shall make things hum for the enemy. • It isn't Christmas weather at all out here, rain and nothing else, but, never mind, we have now got used to it. Every good wish to all inquirers at home." In another letter written to his mother he states.:—

"Just a line at this festive season to remind you I am still well, which is something to be thankful for, considering the conditions.. Do not wbrry about irte, for, after all, I am com- forted by the thought that I am only doing my duty, and it is a pleasure to know that our loved ones at home are safe from the brutality of the Germans: That is what we are fighting for, our King and country, our dear mothers and fathers, sisters and brothers, and all those whom we hold near and dear. All of us out here are looking forward to the time when we can come home again, and when we do we shall be better young men in every way, for this is the life that'will turn many of us from bad to good. War has taught many of us a lesson, but I. am hoping God will pull us through and then we shall he able to return to ease all the aching hearts."

The Lord Mayor of London had written to the Lord Mayors and Mayors in the country suggesting that they should attend the Parish Church offi- cially on that day, and the committee asked the Chairman of the Lymm Council .to attend 'the Parish Church, together with the members of the Council. The writer added: "We feel sure that your recognition of the day and the collec- tion would do much good to help our own sick • and wounded men."

The Chairman invited the members of the Council to meet him at 10.15 on Sunday morning to proceed to the Parish Church.

IST [CT • 3

No New Gas Cookers.—At the meeting of the Lymm Gas Committee on December 20th it was decided that no new gas stoves, cookers, or con- nections should be supplied during the present crisis owing to the shortage of coal.—This course was approved • by the Council on Thursday.

Council Chairman's Good Wishes.—At the conclusion of the Council meeting on Thursday, the• Chairman (Mr. H. H. Gotobed) said: "This is the last meeting of the Old Year, and I would like to express my good wishes to every member of the Council and to the officials and to the representatives of the Press. May the anniver- sary of this meeting be in times of peace."

" I ndispensable."—At a meeting of the Lymm General Purposes Commitee, held on December 20th, it was unanimously decided that an appeal should be made to thealoc- al Recruiting Tribunal to release Mr. Cecil Mort (assistant to the Clerk of the Council) from military duties on the ground that the committee consider kis ser- vices indispensable.—This ,was approved by the Council at their meeting on Thursday. -

Congregational Church.—Watehnight services were held in the Congregational Church last night, a large congregation attending the last services of the old year. Prayers for the sol- diers were raised by the Rev., S. Gill, and a minute before midnight the worshippers engaged in silent prayer, the solemnity of the occasion being very manifest, especially in view of the present crisis.

At the Council Meeting.—The membtrs pres- ent at the meeting of the District Council on Thursday evening were: Messrs, R. H. Gotobed (in the chair), J. II Holt (vice-chairman), W. G. Groves, C. E. Cowper, S. Critchley and F. Lockyer. The officials present were: Messrs. W. Mallard (clerk and collector), Cecil Mort (assist- ant clerk), Dr. Leonard Thorp (deputy medical officer), D. Mort (surveyor), T. H. West . (gas manager), and C. J. Barnes (water manager).

A Councillor's Bereavement—Before com- mencing the business of the Council at Thurs- day evening's meeting. the Chairman (Mr. R. H. Gotobed) expressed the sympathy of the Council with Councillor T. IL Warburton in the loss that he has sustained by the death of two of his children as a result of infections disease. He moved that a Message of condolence should be sent to him.—This was seconded by Councillor Holt and supported by Councillor Critchley, and adopted.

Council Finances.—Couneillor Cowper. at the Council meeting all Thursday evening, said that the Council seemed to be in a muddle with regard to financial matters, and suggested that an Advisory Committee should be formed to make inquiries and to formulate suggestions.— The Chairman: I have suggestedlhat we should ,have a sub-committee to consider the question of finance.—A sub-committee of the Finance Committee is to be formed to go into the matter.

Lecture on Serbia.:—On Wednesday, January 12th, at three o'clock. Mrs. Ridgway is to deliver a lecture on that gallant and hardy race of people in the Balkans, the Serbians. Very few are intimate with the doings and customs of thig Eastern race except that they know its country- men. have made a great stand 'against over- whelming adds, .and therefore the inhabitants are assured of the lecture being not only in- teresting, but educational and instructive. Lyn:tm Grammar School has been placed at the lecturer's disposal, and no charge for admission is .being made.

To Deal With Unsatisfactory Private Roads.— At the meeting a the District Council on Thurs- day evening, Councillor Holt moved: "That sub-committee be appointed to take into con- sideration the adoption of the 'Public Health Acts Amendment Act, 1,907." Councillor Holt explained that if the Act were adopted it would give the Council powers they did not now pos- sess, and enable them, among other things, to deal with unsatisfactory private roads. The Council. added the speaker, ought to have pos- sessed these powers long ago.—The resolution was seconded and carried unanimously.

Charity Football Match for Local Soldiers.— Lyn= and Oughtrington.Football Clubs met at Oughtrington. on Boxing Day." Unfortunately, the weather was wretched; and in consequence the attendance .w.a.tr sawn. I:Dothan of high Merit was out of. the question, owing to. the lieliVy state; of the-ground and wretched:.condi bons. Play on the whole- was pretty even until fifteen minutes from the close; when Oughtring- ton gradually asserted themselves and ran out victors to the tune of seven goals to three. .There were no players of outstanding -note on :either side. However, a return fixture is being arranged, when it is hoped better conditions will prevail and a better exhibition given. The match was originated with a.view of providing cigarettes, &c., for local soldiers.

Letters from the Trenches.—Sergt. J. H. Mos- ton, of the local." Terrier-," in a -letter to his relatives, dated December 24th; 1915, states: "Just a line to you, as it is the day before Christmas, to let you know I am still safe and sound. Out here it isn't like Christmas weather at all, for it is nearly always raining, and we have to sleep in our wet clothes, which, to say the least of it, is decidedly uncomfortable. In the trenches the .boys are lip to the waist in water and mud., so you see what we have to con- tend with. We literally have to dig ourselves out after being in for a few hours, yet one can- not help but laugh, 'for it is really funny.. W- are 'expecting to have a good time to-xnorrov yet I suppose at times our thoughts will stray t those at. home and of .what • they are doing till it is all part of the game, and we must put our shoulders to the wheel to drive out the enemy. However, I' hope that by next Christ- mas we shall all be home again."

MILITARY WEDDING.

Gallant "Tommy" Makes Good Use

Of Short Leave.

The marriage of Private John Read, of the Lyruin .Territorials, and .Miss Alice. Whitfield, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Whitfield, of Branch Cottages, Cherry-lane, was solemn- ised at the Parish Church on Boxing Day by the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston). The bride, who was given away by her. father, was charmingly attired in a costume of blue serge, with hat to match. Her only bridesmaid was Kitty Clarke, a winsome little girl, who looked bewitching in a dress suitable for the occasion. The hest man was Lance-Corporal Ernest Hadden, of the same regiment as the bride- groom, who is also on a short leave.

The service was choral, the hymns being sung -by a large congregation with fervour. The bridegroom, whose private address is at Rush Green, is well known in the district. Previous to enlistment at the outbreak of war, he was employed by the London and North-Western Railway Company. He was tn his first short leave last week-end and returned to duty on Thursday morning. After the ceremony a reception was held at the Crown Hotel, at which about one hundred guest's sat down to a substantial repast. The bride and bridegroom have had extended to them many hearty congratulations and num- erous beautiful wedding presents have been received.

LYIIII RECRUITING.

The Help Given By The Volunteer Regiment.

To the Editor of "The Warrington Examiner."

Sir,—With reference to Mr. Watkin's letter of thanks to the voluntary workers in connec- tion with Lord Derby's Recruitinc, Scheme, pub- lished last week, quite unwittingly, I am sure, Mr. Watkin omitted to mention the splendid work of. Mr. G. R. Williamson, platoon com- mander; Mr. F. Garside; hon. secretary; and other members of the Lymm detachment of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment. Had it not been for the willing sacrifices and valuable, business abilities of these gentlemen, it would not have been possible for one to have coped with the work satisfactorily. One of the first tenets of the Volunteer Regiment is to assist with recruit- ing, and I can honestly say that the Lymm and Oughtrington detachments have fully justified their existence under this important heading, and for their assistance I am deeply grateful.— Yours,:•&c.,

FRED E. EVANS, Recruiting Officer.

Lyxam, Dec. 27th, 1915.

IN BRITISH EAST AFRICA.

Letter From Councillor Cowper's Son.

A graphic letter, describing his experiences in British East Africa, has been received by Coun- cillor Cowper from his son, Mr. Charles Cowper, who, writing from Kilindini, says :—. • " The. Germans made an attempt to blow up. the line about.four miles from this camp during. the night but were driven off. The enemy bumped 'Accidentally into one of °lir piquets. which opened _rapid fire. They waited 'until about '5O Germans came into the open and then fired. The enemy turned and fled, and our men then retreated to a position- 100 yards lower. down. The enemy, • when they had collected themselves, advanced again on to the piquet's old position, only to be fired on again. Just when they had discovered our men the armoured train rolled up and gave them 'beano,' at which they retreated.. We found one dead German askaris, and'several bandoliers, small barrels of water, &c., but there is no doubt that we Wounded several, and as the bush has not been reached yet, we hope to find some more dead. Our casualties were—nil. Of course, all this happened at midnight. Rather funny, I was out with 35 men in the afternoon, acting as 4 covering party to a gang of workmen. Thank you so' much for the cigarettes; which arrived safe and sound; your • letter advising same did not arrive for over a fortnight later; I cannot make out where it was delayed." Writing later Mr. Cowper says:— _ _

"The .Germans made ;six attempts to blow up the line Some 50 miles up .the Nairobs . line from Voi, but did very.li4le damage. To-day it has been raining in torrents, • but as our camp has .been well irrigated, nobody is any the worse. "CHARLIE."

===== 0002601_19160101_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
DIPHTHERIA AGAIN.

Fresh Outbreak At Oughtringlon.

TWO DEATHS.

Diphtheria has •broken out again in Lymm, this time, we regret to say, with fatal results. At a meeting of the District Council on Thurs- day evening, Dr. Leonard Thorp (the temporary medical officer) reported that after nearly a month's freedom from diphtheria, a case was notified at Oughtrington on December 4th, and since that day seven other cases, the last on Friday, December 24th, had been reported. They had thus had eight cases occurring in children attending the Oughtrington schools. Owing to the occupation of the hospital with other cases he had been unable to offer isolation to the fresh five cases, but satisfactory arrange- ments were made by the medical attendants for treating the cases at home. With the third case such arrangements were not possible, and by commandeering the nurses' bedroom and making it into a ward, he took this case into the Isolatian Hospital. In two of the other cases it was not necessary to take the patients into hospital, as the home isolation was satis- factory. In one house where it would have been, of all the cases, the most desirable to receive the case in the isolation hospital, such an offer was definitely refused, the patient not being willing for the removal. •

===== 0002601_19160101_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
Fatal Cases.

He regretted to have to report that two cases proved fatal. One child died a few days later at home. The other child was very ill and con- sent was given to get a trained nurse. On the following day the parents agreed to the child going into the hospital if the nurse would accompany it. - This child, he regretted to state, died in hospital that (Thursday) mornihg. Origin of the Outbreak.

The most careful inquiries, continued the doctor, have again failed to find the origin of this fresh outbreak. The first outbreak was con- fined to the Lymm and Statham schools. The second . outbreak has been confined to the. Oughtrington school. Nothing common to thw. cases can be fixed upon. As the Surveyor will report to you, the drains at Oughtrington school are not quite satisfactory, and should be immediately altered, considering the number of children attending the schools and the wide area from which they come. The number of privy closets in the district and in most cases their close proximity to the dwelling-house is most undesirable, and demands urgent atten- tion and alteration. In one house, which it was my intention to .bring fully to your notice this evening, but feel compelled under the circum- stances to leave until your next meeting, the position of the closet, the drainage—l should rather say, the want of drainage—and the damp- ness-of the house render the same as one which will have to be dealt with without delay, as I consider the house unfit for habitation. The Condition of the Roads,

For economical reasons alone I desire to draw your attention and ask for somethinglo be done straight away in altering :the condition of the roadway in the Rookery,.New Field West, Hyde. Qldfield-road and Carlton-road. These 'roads in wet weather are almost impassable, and• for days after hold collections of mud . and water, most lovely breeding grounds for all varieties of disease. In each of the five mentioned roads cases have occurred during the present outbreak of disease.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
The Hospital.

Again I draw your attention to the hospital. We had this month to take over the nurses' bedroom and were treating three different diseases in a hospital most inconveniently con- structed for the nursing of two diseases. Yesterday (Wednesday), when a child was so ill, there was no room in which to place it and to allow the parents to be with it, and I was compelled to allow them the general ward, which might have been impossible if ,the hospital hail been more fully occupied. The Chairman (Mr. Gotobed) said that they all regretted very much this second outbreak. They had hoped that they had got rid of this disease. Ile felt sure, however, that the Medical Officer and Mr. Mort (the surveyor) would do all that they could to bring it to an end..

===== 0002601_19160101_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
FEARNHEAD.

Christmas Gathering.—The annual tea and concert was held in the schoolroom of the Risley Independent "Methodist Church on Christmas Day, and • provided a very enjoyable evening. Commencing with a free tea to all the Sunday school scholars, followed by an excellent tea for adults, a miscellaneous programme was given, presided over by Mr. W. Evans. Songs were well rendered by Miss C. Barton and Miss Ball. Mr. Bibby (humorist) delighted bile audi- ence by his songs. Recitations were given by Miss A. Tomlin*, Miss B. Green, Mr. D. Ella.m, and the 'Sunday school scholars. Great credit is due to Messrs:. Cook. Bold and Higham for the action song entitled "Urchins Wee.".' An action song, entitled "Boys versus Girls;'.' was also given by the Sunday school scholars.'..Dia- logues, entitled " Ilardup Rivals," "Ferrell's Fix," and "The Little Scamp 'Next Door," were given by the 'young people. The accompanists were Miss G. Hughes and Mr. P. Bates. .`

ORFORD.

Carol Service.—The evening serviceon Sunday at the Parish Church took the form of a carol service, as is usual on the Sunday after Christ- mas. The service was conducted by the Vicar, the Rev. G. Vale Owen. The preacher was the Rev. W. A. Gibson (Holloway). The service opened with the hymn "Christians, awake;" being sung as a processional. The carols in- cluded "Come, all ye thankful," "How beauti- ful upon the mountains," "Good Christian men, rejoice," "Brightest and best," "Good King Winceslas," "Once in Royal David's City," and' "Angels ever bright and fair." The solos were sung by Thomas Walker and John Ellis. Miss E. Brownhill and Mr. H. Brownhill rendered the duet, "Love divine." At the close of the service the National Anthem was 'sung, the recessional being "_While shepherds .watched." _ _

The junior members of the choir, went out carol singing, and collected the sum oLX2, which has been forwarded to Mr. W. B. Randall, 7, Hanover-street, Warrington, secretary of the fund for blind soldiers and sailors, as a contri- bution to that fund.

STOCKTON HEATH.

For Our Soldiers.—A most successful whist drive was held at the Adult School on Monday evening. Nineteen tables were utilised, and the prizes were won as follows :—Ladies: 1, Miss Cade; 2, Mrs. E. Lightfoot; 3, Mrs. W. Mann. Gentlemen: 1, Mrs. J. Bailey; 2, Mrs. T. Isher- wood (both these ladies played a 'gentleman's hand); 3, Mr. F. Cox. The " hidden " prizes were won by Mrs. Isherwood and Mr. A. Morri- son. Mr. W. H. Sherburn was the M.C. The whist drive had been arranged for the benefit of our soldiers, and the proceeds; which amounted to £4 Gs., will be divided between Winwick Hospital and the Bold-street Soldiers' Home. •

St.•Thomas' Band of Hope.—The annual treat for the children of the Band of Hope took place on 'Thursday evening, and, as in former years, was provided by Mr. Frank Houghton, of Apple- ton. The children spent a very happy time, thanks to the kindness of numerous friends, one of whom (Mrs. Milling) visited the treat, and was -warmly welcomed. A vote of thanks was accorded Mr. and- Mrs. F. Houghton and other friends, on the motion of Mr. Charles Hallows. Mr. Houghton suitably responded, and "gave the children some good advice, also remarking that they would not have had .so good a treat if it were not for the workers and especially their leader, Mr. J. Hallows. After singing the National Anthem the children went home satis- fied with a "real good treat." Later in the evening the workers indulged in games, compe- titions, &c., and spent a happy • time together. The Superintendent (Mr. John Hallows) eulo- gised the efforts of his faithful band of workers, and especially those of the secretary, Mr. A. Stannard, and Mr. Wightman, deputy superin- tendent. The party dispersed about 10.30, after singing "Auld Lang Syne " and the National Anthem.

, Hill Cliffe Baptist Sunday School—On Box- ing day the annual party was held in the school- room.. The room had been tastefully decorated by the young 'people. A large party of children and their, parents sat down to .the .ample tea provided, after which .a concert was given by 'the scholars 'and friends. The Rev. G. Curtis presided, and he was supported by -Mr. J. Moss (superintendent). The programme consisted of songs by Misses Capell, Gladys Pemberton, Evelyn.. Frazer and Bertha Langley; duet by Mrs. Roughsedge and Mrs. Ault; recitations by, lEva Phillips, Edith Phillips, Miss Emlin, Mr. Whitfield, Phylis Moss, Domoni Moss, Hilda Holland; Miss Emlin 'and Miss Lillie Byfield gave a sketch, which was greatly appreciated. The Girls' Guild, under the leadership of Mrs. Roughsedge and Miss Dodd, gave several character songs during the evening. The secretary's report. read by Mr. Fraser, showed that in spite of so many having left the school at the country's bidding, they had a good balance, and the good work was still going on. The .prizes were distributed to the scholars by Miss L. Grounds and Mr. -J. Moss. During the evening Mr. Pemberton, Mrs. Roughsedge, Miss Challoner and Miss Florrie Byfield accompanied at the piano. Oranges were presented to the scholars by ;Mr. Roughsedge and Mr. J. , Moss. The National Anthem brought this happy even- ing to a close. .

Tea For Soldiers' Wives and Children.—The tea for soldiers' wives and children, organised , by the St. Thomas' Band of Hope, and held on Boxing Day in the Parish Room, which had been nicely decorated, was a complete success, thanks to the generosity of Mrs. Lyon, Mrs. Parr, Mrs. V. Davies, Mrs. Cooper, Mrs. Mill- ing, Mrs. .Segar Owen, Mrs. Bedell, Mrs. , Parkinson, Mrs. Lancefield, Miss Heath, Mr. L. Davies and Mr. Mynekyme, and .also the com- mittee of .the soldiers' mothers' meeting, who !kindly sent a supply of mince pies. An excel- lent tea 'was provided, which was served by the workers. After tea some romping games were played, in Which the young folk thoroughly enjoyed themselves. Fruit and cake were handed round at intervals, and while the children were enjoying themselves with the crackers kindly provided by the Vicar, who was unable to be present, the mothers, joined in several competitions for prizes kindly given by one of the ladies. :The Christmas tree, laden with prizes, was lighted at 6.30, and this. was a source of pleasure to all. In the unavoidable absence of Mrs. Lyon, who had kindly pro- vided the gifts, Mrs. Segar Owen, acting on her behalf, distributed them. -Speaking to the wives and children in a cheerful manner, she expressed a hope that their loved on would soon be home to cheer their hearts and homes. (Applause.) The Rev'. E. W. Clarke thanked Mrs. Owen for her kindness in coming to dis- tribute the presents. On the motion of Mr. Wightman, seconded by Mr. C. Hallows, o hearty vote of thanks was accorded to all who had assisted with the party. The wives and children shortly after left for home, and as each one left the room they .were given a packet of sweets, a packet of nuts, a mince pie and an orange. The mothers were also given a box of chocolate. The workers heartily appreciated a number of nicely and appropriately decorated cakes which had been given by kind friends: The cakes were greatly enjoyed.

RUNCORN RURAL COUNCIL.

iVinthly Meetings instead Of Fortnightly.

CHEAPER GAS?

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Connell was held at the Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. Thomas Antwis presiding. Mr. Woodward said that at the next meeting he would move that monthly Meetings of the Council. be held Instead of fortnightly.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
The Branding of Calves.

A circular letter was read from the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries inquiring if any action had been taken by the Council to secure the branding of calves at auction markets, to prevent the illegal branding of calves by per- sons other than the auctioneer himself or his representatives, and to prevent the slaughter of unbranded calves under six months old, which are not out of cows of the dairy breeds exempted under the Maintenance of Live Stock Order of 1910.

It was pointed out that the Act had little bearing on the district. Clocks for Gas Lamps.

Regarding the street lighting of Stockton Heath, Latchford Without, Grappenhall, Wal- ton, and Appleton, the Warrington Corporation Gas Engineer wrote stating that as more than half of the lamplighters had enrolled under the Lord Derby scheme, they had proposed to fix clocks for lighting and extinguishing the lamps. The saving in lamplighters! wages would en- able the Corporation to quote, a lower price than at present.
