RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

Drastic Suggestion By The Local Government Board.

WAR-TIME SAVINGS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Guardians was held on Tuesday at the Dutton Workhouse, Mr. J. L. Jones in the chair.

SOLDIERS' DEPENDENTS.

A circular letter had been forwarded by the Local Government Board, asking that they should be notified if the wife, child or other dependent of any soldier became chargeable to the Guardians.

The appointment of Mrs. Hudson, wife of the acting master of the House, as assistant nurse to the Union at a salary of £25 per annum, sub- ject to a report by the medical officer, at the end of three months, as to the way in which she had discharged her duties, was agreed to by the Local Government Board. A letter from the Local Government Board was read, stating that owing to the large num- beer of men who had enlisted, they suggested a curtailment of the ordinary methods of administration. Included in the suggestions were :—Monthly returns of pauperism instead- of weekly, fortnightly or monthly returns of inmates instead of weekly, the substitution of monthly for the fortnightly meetings of the Guardians, and a number of other alterations in regard to details of administration. Mr. Linaker said that he would give notice that at the next meeting he would move that the Board meet monthly instead of fortnighly.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
TREATS FOR CHILDREN.

The Acting Clerk reported that Lady Harmood Banner had offered to give a Christmas tree and treat to the children at the Children's Home in conjunction with all the children at Dutton village. A suitable date would be fixed. Mr. Spruce said that the Ladies' Visiting Committee had also made arrangements to'pro- vide a Christmas tree and treat prior to Lady Banner's offer.

Lady ,Banner was thanked by the Board for gifts of evergreens for decorating the house, and Mrs. Armitage was also thanked for her gift of Christmas cards ,for the inmates.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPLIMENTS OF THE SEASON.

The Chairman wished all the compliments of the season, and said he hoped that all would have a happy and prosperous Now Year, and that the war would soon end.

WAR PRISONERS' ADVENTURES.

Eight Russians Reach England.

Eight Russian soldiers, who were taken pri- soners by ,the Germans and who have escaped from different camps, arrived in England on Thursday night, having successfully evaded all attempts at recapture. They met not knowing that each of the others had escaped, and reached England on the same boat.

- - Two Germans Nearly Suffocated As Stowaways. Two German soldiers were landed at Queens. town on Thursday from the British steamship Lord Erne, who were found aboard that vessel as stowaways. The steamer was proceeding from Havre to the United States, and when going down Channel two men were discovered in the hold in an almost suffOcated condition. On being brought on deck they were found to be young German soldiers, war prisoners, who had succeeded in getting aboard the steamer at Havre in an endeavour as stowaways to reach a neutral country. Soon after being discovered a:British warship was sighted and the facts communicated. Her commander ordered the Lord Erne to proceed to Queenstown and land the men there. They ,will now be interned in 'lreland.

THRIFT SCHEMES.

Safe And Popular Means Of

Investment.

Last week we reproduced a letter on Govern- ment and Thrift, which had been sent by kr. Edmund Osborne to the "Manchester Guardian." In the same contemporary last Saturday the following letter appeared in con- tinuation of the subject:—

===== 0002601_19160101_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR LOAN. El Bonds Not Popular.

Sir,—With all due deference to Mr. Drum- mond Fraser, I am afraid the Government will hot "tempt the labouring classes to save" by offering them, as he suggests, "a 1:1 war bond in the form of a card divided into twenty spaces to be filled up by weekly instalments with stamps costing is. each, and when the twenty' payments are completed the El to be transferred to a war-bond pass-book." A plan similar to the one he outlines was devised by the originator of the Post Office Savings Bank for t,he purpose of penny savings. The scheme was put into operation 35 years ago, and for a time was suc- cessful—particularly among children. But the novelty has worn off, and now that a small pass- book has been introduced by penny savings banks "licking stamps" is not even popular amongst children. One can scarcely imagine a plan which has become unpopular with chil- dren, and being resuscitated "to tempt." their parents, meeting with any degree of succcess. It is to be hoped that the official mind is not already made up to issue El bonds, repayable in five years, because they are not popular. nowing that their employment is vicarious, the working classes desire to be free to fix the term of investment most suitable to their individual needs. If "unforeseen circumstances" arise, and it is imperative for them to fall back upon their savings, they must be in a position to withdraw all, or a portion, on demand or at short notice. The only way to Meet that is by the issue of a War Loan pass-book wherein their deposits may be recorded at once. Savings banks have been the means of pro- viding the people with a safe and popular means of investment, and, as I have previously pointed out, if the limit as to deposits is increased and a higher rate of interest given, they could accomplish all that is desired. If it is to encourage thrift as well as to find money for the Treasury, a scheme which would appeal to the working classes must take into account the peculiarity of their position. First, get their names on the books of the bank, then give them an opportunity to invest their surplus by an easy method of transferring from an ordinary to a War Loan pass-book. And if, say, for one month's notice of withdrawal they could get 3 per cent.. interest, for three months', notice 4 per Cent, interest, and six -months' , notice 5 per cent. interest, an incentive to save would be given, together with a desire to allow their money to remain so as to get the highest rate of interest. The Committee will have to differentiate be- tween those who have already a "banking account" and those who have up to now been shy. As the latter have more than an academic acquaintance with "the irreducible minimum" and its effects, they must be assured that their savings are " safe " and " getatable." They have never been properly approached before, and the present offers such a splendid oppor- tunity that it would be a pity if it were lost for want of a suitable scheme. One does hope that the Committee will recog- nise the fact that, as you put it in your leader the other day, "the problem is not a financial as much as a psychological one."—Yours, EDMUND OSBORNE. 22, Bold-street, Warrington, December 21. I

SHREWSBURY FLOODS.

People Take Refuge In Their Bedrooms.

Shrewsbury is largely under water, the Severn having risen more rapidly than within living memory. The public bowling greens, tennis courts, and the Quarries are submerged. Many main thoroughfares and hundreds of houses are flooded, and the occupants have taken refuge in the bedrooms.

At dawn on Wednesday, men up to their waist in water, rescued pigs and fowls, hut many were found drowned. Boats were plying in the streets. A cripple, with water six inches from his bed, was rescued.

IN THE COURTS.

PAWNING MILITARY BOOTS.

Margaret McLaughlin, of no settled abode, was remanded for seven days by the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday, on a charge of assisting a soldier to pawn a pair of military boots. Prisoner was dis- covered in a local pawnshop by P.C. McDonald with a pair of military boots in her possession, which she had attempted to pledge. In conse- quence of her unsatisfactory replies she was taken into custody. From inquiries the police had ascertained that a soldier had given the

boots to the accused, but they had been unable to trace him.

A remand for seven days was necessary to complete the inquiries.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
HOUSEBREAKER CAUGHT.

Advised By The Police To "Go Straight "—But Failed.

The housebreaking exploits of James Towey. an ex-convict. will at all events be at an end for the next twelve months, for this was the term of jniprisonment piled out to him at the Police CoUrt on Wednesday by the magistrates —Messrs. J. W. Hayward in the chair, Alder- man Smethurst and Mr. W. T. Williams.

The evidence of the police was that_on the night .of December 20thi nb less-than five houses in the vicinity of Bewsey-street and Rolleston- street were entered by Towey. The charge against him was that he being a convicted thief, was found in the backyard of 26, Rolleston-street under such circum- stances as to suggest he was there with the in- tent to raid the premises. Inspector Hodgkiss, in detailing the facts, said that Towey arrived in Warrington on December 15th, when he called at the Police Station. The Police _Court Missioner gave him financial assistance and some clothes. Two days later he was found work at Bewsey Forge. Later, he again called at the Police Station and was given further assistance and advised to "go straight" and keep away from the drink. Attempting To Get Into Houses.

A further two days had only elapsed when the police heard that he had been attempting to get into a house at the back of 58, Bewsey• street. The house was occupied by Mr. George Miller, who, on going into the yard in the dark, found Towey crouched underneath the window. After forcibly ejecting him, Mr. Miller in- formed the police. After this escape prisoner visited the yard of '46, Bewsey-street, where he stole a shirt. He then turned his attention to the yards at the rear of Rolleston-street.

,Detectiyes Skelton and Green were by this time in the neighbourhood, and they saw Towey emerge from a backyard in Rolleston: street. After a short chase he was captured in Froghall-lane. In the chase a shirt which he had stolen dropped from underneath his coat.

Inspector Ilodgkiss added that Towey was deserving of the full penalty. It was no use trying to reform him. As soon as he was released from serving a term of imprisonment he lapsed into his old criminal ways.

"Too Drunk."

Prisoner said he could not admit the offence, as he was too drunk to remember, anything about it.

Detective Skelton, who proved the arrest, said accused was not too drunk to try and get away It was stated by Inspector Hodgkiss that prisoner had forty-four previous conviction. against him for larceny, frequenting, house- breaking, burglary and vagrancy. The last cnn- viction was in February, 1910, at Chester Assizes, when, on a charge of housebreaking, he was sentenced to three years' penal servitude and, eight years under. the Preventative Deten- tion Act.

The Chairman: We hardly know what to do with you. It seems as if you are incorrigible. You will have to go to gaol for twelve montl4•:, with harbour.

A SAD CASE.

Soldier's AViie AVho Stole In Return

For Kindness.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday, -.Elizabeth Charnock, • wife of Edward Charnock, a Soldier, wag sent to gaol for thre& months on a charge of- stealing a shawl value 3s. 6d., the property of Alice An Johnston, 21, Philip-street. Accused said she took the shawl in mistake for her own.

The Chief Constable pointed out that on the evidence there could be no question of a mis- take ..having been Made. It appeared that at three o'clock on Christmas morning Mrs. John- ston, to her surprise, discovered the prisoner in a drunken condition in the lavatory in the backyard. Prisoner pleaded to be allowed to remain in the house until daylight, as she had nowhere to go. Mrs. Johnston took pity on her and allowed her to sleep on the sofa, where she remained until three o'clock in the afternoon. Later in the day prisoner decamped and took with her a shawl belonging to the prosecutrix, and left behind a dirty, worthless one of her own. The matter was reported to the police, and from inquiries it was ascertained that the prisoner had given the shawl to a woman named Wilson, who had pledged it for her for two shillings. When arrested by Detective Green, prisoner stoutly denied any knowledge of the shawl.

Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., said the prisoner came out of gaol three weeks ago. On her release the War Office instructed him to act as " almoner " in respect of the allow- ance made by the Government to soldiers' wives. During her imprisonment her children were taken to the Workhouse. He had.paid to her various slims of money, and he had tried his best to reform the woman, but he was afraid she did not appreciate anything ,that was done on her behalf. In consequence of her habits he declined to bring the children out of the Workhouse. Only a week ago he saw her in a drunken condition. There were several previous convictions against the woman.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED THEFT OF RUBBER.

At the Borough Police Court yesterday, before the Mayor, Alderman Peacock (in the chair), Aldermen Bolton and Smethurst, Messrs. S. Wright, G. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, T. Locker and W. Furness, a charge of stealing rubber, value 45., from the St. Helens Cable Company, was preferred against William Henry Hayes, last from Liverpool.

Mr. T. S. Steel, who prosecuted, said the pris- oner was only arrested on Thursday, and as it would be necessary to complete a. number of inquiries, a remand of seven days was necessary. Prisoner had only been employed as a casual three days ago, and left his employment with- 'out notice, taking away with him the war button supplied by the firm, who were under Government control.

The application for a remand was granted.

SOLDIER IN TROUBLE.

Charged With Obtaining Money By

False Pretenek.

Private Henry Coddinson, of the Notts and Derby Regiment,, stationed at Derby, was charged at the Warrington Borough Police Court yesterday with obtaining two sums of money by false pre- tences from Frederick Bunch and John Burgess Starkey. The Chief Constable said that the accused was in the Army and had been absent from his unit for two months. He seemed to have been wandering from town to town, calling upon several people representing that he was in some way connected with the Cadet Corps or the , Church Lads' Brigade. In this manner it was alleged he had obtained several sums of money from kindly disposed persons. , On Thursday afternoon he called upon Mr. Frederick Bunch, the assistant works manager at Bewsey Forge. Prisoner informed Mr. Bunch that lie was a chauffeur, and that his master's car had broken down and was in the course of repair at a local motor garage. He represented that he was a member of the Church Lads' Brigade, and on his statement ,Mr. Bunch advanced him three shillings. On prisoner leaving the works Mr. Bunch telephoned to the firm at which prisoner had alleged the motor was being repaired and discovered that it was a concocted yarn. He immediately mounted his bicycle and proceeding along Bewsey-road saw the prisoner in conversa=

Good "Gates" And Good Foot- ball For The Holidays.

The handling code still proves to be an at- traction for holiday-makers, and this was so last week-end. On Christmas Day nine matches were played, and these were witnessed by 65,000 spectators. Though the attendances on Boxing Day were not so good—the bad weather being responsible—they were more than sufficient to justify the games being played. Of the Christmas Day matches an interesting feature was the defeat of Oldham by Swinton, on the former's ground. Another was the vic- tory of Bradford over Rochdale.Hornets, on the latter's groimd. Broughton''' Rangers were beaten for the second time this season by Salford, while Barrow were badly beaten by Wigan at Central IPark. The two St. Helens clubs played for eighty minutes without scorin,t a single point. , There were eight matches played on Boxing Day. Swinton's winning career was checked at Salford, where, however, they accomplished a good performance in sharing sixteen points. The results of the two days' matches were Christmas Day.

INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

Excellent Matches During The Holidays.

Intermediate League football was in full swing during the Christmas holidays, and some good matches were .witnessed. A big surprise occurred on Christmas Day at Wilderspool, when, following upon their defeat ,at the hands ,of Parochial last week, Heathsido had again to bow the knee, this time to St. Thomas', by five points to three. Parochial were successful in their match with Rylands's by five points to two, while Beamont triumphed over Bolton by a penalty goal to nil, At Wilderspool on Monday a very fast game between Parochial.- and Beamont resulted in a draw of three points each, a feature of the game being the magnificent defence of the Beamout lads. Sacred Heart—a much improved team—beat St. Thomas' by six points to five, while a weak St. James' team were mastered by St. Mary's by three points to nil.

CHRISTMAS DAY.

GOOD MATCH BETWEEN BOLTON AND

BEAM ONT.

- Bolton and Beamont met at Wilderspool in the presence Of a good crowd. Bolton started, and were immediately on the attack, but a good punt by a. Beamont threequarter relieved, the pressure and removed play to midfield. • Here a scrimmage was formed, and Bolton came away with a good. rush. One of their players intercepted a Beamont pass, and evading two players, seemed a likely scorer, but he fell, and so lost the chance. By good forward work, Beamont removed play to the other end of the field, where some desperate scrimMages were fought out. Finally Bolton were penalised, and from an easy position Witton gave his side the lead. From the 'kick-out Beamont were soon again on the attack, but a free-kick was awarded to Bolton for obstruction. Beamont had scion to act- on the defensive, and Witton marked. Bolton came again, but Barnett picked up splendidly and found touch with a good kick. Later, Bolton and Beamont were awarded free-kicks, but nothing tangible was recorded in either case, and the final score was Beamont, two points; Bolton, nil. The forwards on both sides played well, while Hill stood out prominently. in the Beamont threequa rter-line. SACRED HEART'S EASY WIN.

In their match with Sacred Heart at Victoria Park, St. James' were very badly represented, several of their .regular players being absent. Thus it was no surprise to see them get beaten by twenty-five points to nil by the much im-

IN THE COURTS.—Continued.

HEATHSIDE AGAIN LOSE.

It was generally evected that HeathMde would be victorious in their match with St. Thomas' at Wilderspool, on Christmas Day. From the commencement of the game, however, St. Thomas' played with much dash and vigour, and when the whistle sounded they had gained a fine victory by five points to three.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
RYLANDS'S GO DOWN.

The Parochial v. Rylands's match, played at Wilderspool on Saturday, proved to be a very stirring one, and was greatly enjoyed by a good number of spectators. Each side had a fair share of the game, and when tl., final whistle sounded Parochial had won by five points to Rylands's two points.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
BOXING DAY.

PAROCHIAL AND BEAMONT DRAW.

On a sodden ground, and in a continual down- pour of rain, Beamont and Parochial met on the '-Wilderspool ground. Beamont won the toss, and elected to play with the wind. They started in fine fashion, and were soon hammer- ing at • the Parochial line, but a good kick relieved the pressure. Not to be denied, Beamont came again, and from a good forward rush, a -.try: was recorded. Percival, however, failed to convert. From the centre-kick, the Parochial • forwards, headed by' Stretford; , made a fine rush, but Jones relieved. Parochial, however, attacked again, and one of their players put in a high kick and followed up. Instead of going forward to .catch the ball,. the Beamont full-back waited for the bounce, with the result- that- the -Parochial players, were soon upsn him and the ball was dribbled over the line and a try resulted. The kick at goal failed. After half-time, the Beamont team were slightly rearranged, Hill taking up the full-back position,' at which he played a fine game. He *as frequently applauded for his sure "catching and fine: kicking. Beamont were on the- defensive, but''.defended their lines mag- nificently. The forwards all played well, giving their backs numerous, opportunities, and even when the ball came into the hands of the Parochial , players, the latter were completely smothered before they could . get their: passing movements going. At-the call of time the result was: Beamont, one try, three points; Parochial. one try, three points.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. MARY'S THREE-POINT WIN.

St. James' ,fielded a weak team against St. Mary's at Wilderspool, and lost by three points to nil. The ground was in a very bad state, and kick and rush was the; order, , very few passing movements being attempted. Each line underwent a severe bombardment in the first half, but nothing resulted, and at half-time the score sheet was blank. Resuming, St. Mary's early got to business, and from a high kick their left-wing seized the ball, and running strongly, beat several of the opposing players and scored the first and last try of the game. The kick at goal was a failure. From now to the end St. Mary's were on the attack, but the St. James' defence held good.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
SACRED HEART'S SECOND HOLIDAY

WIN.

Fresh from their smart victory over Heath- side on Christmas Day, St. Thomas' entered the arena at Wilderspool on Monday- feeling very confident of adding another victory to their credit. Sacred Heart, however, is a team to be reckoned with, and at the close of. the game the score stood in their favour by six points to five. The. contest proved to be very, even and exciting, with Sacred Heart just about the better team. The game started before a good crowd in at- tendance. Sacred Heart facing a heavy wind, were soon on the attack, but were driven back by a long punt from the Stockton Heath custodian. The " Hearts' " right-centre obtained possession and passed out to his wing man, who carried the game to midfield, where a scrummage was formed. Play was again brought near the " Hearts' " line, where Wibley gathered up and scored the first points for Stockton- Heath, a goal resulting. Half-time eame with the score 5-6 in favour of St. Thomas'.

After the interval the " Hearts " put more spirit and dash into their game, and were soon going strong for the opposite line. Dutton and Plant dribbling along the wing., sent the ball over the St. Thomas' line, but Lloyd was there first, and purporting to dead the ball, put his foot on it. Dutton was the smarter of the two, for he ran up and with a great dive scored the " Hearts' " first try, the goal-kick being a failure. St., Thomas' again pressed hard, but Clarke found touch with a long punt. Farring- ton obtained the ball and passed to Baker, who ran down the line; but was forced into touch. Callon received a pass and sent along to Grady who, in his turn, gave to Baker, for that player to register the " Hearts'" second try seven minutes from time. After this the game was give and take, and there was no good result from either side.

For the " Hearts " Callon, Dutton. Clarke and Baker (who has scored six tries in his last two matches), played good games, whilst Wibley, Lloyd and Fitzgerald were the , pick of St. Thomas'.

The St. Thomas' pack were much the heavier. but not the better, as the "Hearts" hooked the bailout nine times out.of ten.

The final score was six points to five in favour of Sacred Heart, who are rising in the League by leaps and bounds, and have been very un- lucky in the past, for in some matches they have only been beaten by an odd point—as they won on this occasion.

ASSOC IATION.

Holiday Disappointments At

===== 0002601_19160101_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
H ood-lane.

WARRINGTON AND Ms"' RICT LEAGUE.

TABLE TO DATE. P. W. L. D. F. A. P. Paddington Hollins Green ' 4 4 0 0 14 2 8 Warrington Central Elmwood Cong. British Alum.

The football fixture for the Hood Lane-ground, for Christmas Day between the Warrington Association and the Kirkdale Club, was a great disappointment through/ the non-appear- ance of the Kirkdale team.: Owing to the repu- tation the latter have, gained by being un- defeated in the Liverpool Central Combination League, and also the fact that, the Sankey men had suffered a great.reverse the previous week, the club supporters turned up in strong force, fully expecting to iwitness a keen game. They waited patiently long after the advertised time to commence. Eventually one of the club officials returned from the station and reported that the Kirkdale players had not travelled, and no game could be played. From a financial

point it will be a great loss to the Warrington A.F.C.

For their Boxing Day fixture a strong team from the Cable Works had been selected, and intluded -Cartwright, of Manchester City, .to oppose the • home team, but misfortune still folleWed them, and at the appointed time for the kick-off only two of the visitors had arrived. Even if both teams had been ready for the game the weather clerk was in a very bad humour, and it was not really fit to play.

On Christmas morning the Sankey. Athletic had the Warrington Central as visitors to their ground. The home team, who are making a reputation for themskves this season, turned out at full strength, but the visitors were not in the same position. Five of their, regular players were absent. The Athletic had the best of the exchanges in the first half, but failed to pierce the visitors' defence. The second por- tion was more keenly contested, and the Athletic gained the first point by one of the ' visitors' full-backs doing the needful for them. A few minutes later the visitors eaualised after some good open play. The game resulted in a draw of one goal each, and thus the Athletic suc- ceeded in maintaining their record. The regu- lar players of the visitors' team did not play up to their usual standard.

Originally there were no fixtures arranged for C tristmas holidays in the Warrington and Distrie, Junior League, but upon the resignation,of the Bewsey Albions an application was received from the Penketh Albions F.C. to fill the vacancy. The hon. secretary accepted this, and made arrangements 'for them to play two games on Christmas Day and two on Boxing Day, so as to enable them to get some of the fixtures fulfilled.

The Penketh Albion F.C. made a very success- ful entry into the Junior League on Christmas Day, playing the Howley United in the morn- ing and Orford Juniors in the afternoon. In both games they succeeded in gaining the full points, defeating the United after a hard and strenuous - game by One goal against none; and in the afttornoon's game against the Juniors they gained a more decisive victory by three goals against one in their favour.

On' Boxing Day morning, on the Arpley grounds, the I'enketh Albion- and - Mersey Juniors opposed each other, before a good.crowd of spectators, and -a very ,keen, game was wit- nessed. The Juniors . were in good trim, and,, playing in surprising fashion,eld..the lead at the interval. In the second portion' they in- creased their advantage from a penalty-kick; and a few minutes before the close of play the visitors missed -scoring from a penalty. The Juniors' supporters were in high glee over their favourites' success.

On Boxing Day the St. Elphit's having no League engagement, arranged to ,play the Emmanuel Institute a: friendly in the Park. The game proved very one-sided . in, the first half, and the Institute rubbed it in, 'penetrating the " Saints' " defence on six occasions. The second half was very even, each 'side scoring once:

The result of this match provided much enjoyment to players of some of the other teams in the League, and the expression which was heard most frequently was Have you seen the ' Saints ' in the Park ?"

Stockton Heath Scouts 9 6 3 0 18 12 12 Howley- United -Orford Juniors Mersey Juniors Penketh Albion.; Bewsey A.F.C. Latchford C.C. Padgate C.L.B.

Having got to the half-way house for the present season from the above League Chart it will be seen that the St. Elphin's have played very consistently, and established a _good lead at the head of the other clubs.

4 The -Fairfield. United. Reserves a . few weeks back disappointed their supporters by failing to defeat the Saints " ' for premier position when they had their strongest team together, which included Woodhouse, of last season's first eleven. -

The Stockton Heath Scouts at. One time 'Chal- lenged the "Saints" very keenly for chief honours, but latterly have failed to maintain their earlier form.

Penketh Anions, the newest corners to the League, made a very strong entry, playing two games on Christmas Day and captured the full points.

But on Boxing Day morning the Mersey Juniors gave. them a surprise and defeated:them by two clear goals.

The Mersey Juniors are a bard lot to beat when playing on their favourite pitch. They knOw which way the ball will bounce.

The Padgate C.L.B. have proved the most disappointing team in the League, having failed to gain a solitary point; but they have shown some of the other clubs a good example by stick- ing together and playing their fixtures, when Dame Fortune does not smile upon them.

MERSEY JUNIORS v. PENKETH ALBIONS.

On the Arpiey ground on Boxing Day morn- ing. • Aikenhead set the ball rolling for the Juniors before a good crowd of spectators. The. visitors' goal was quickly attacked, and 'their defence had to exert themselves to the full to. save their citadel. After an occasional visit to the home goal and a spell of midfield play, the Juniors returned to .the attack, and at the -end •of eighteen minutes' play Mac scored with a good shot. The Visitors made a rush on the home goal, which resulted in a corner-kick. This v•was well • • placed, and Walters,- the home custodian, brought off a good clearance. Even 'play followed, and the interval arrived with the scores :—Juniors, 1 goal; Albions, nil. On resuming, the Alb ions at once attacked the home goal and made strong efforts to get on level terms, but Smith, Gray and Walters proved equal to the occasion and saved the situation. From a. goal-kick the home forwards gained possession and transferred play to the opposite• end, where one of the visitors' full-backs handled the ball inside the area. Sinai" was entrusted with the kick, and he safely steered the ball between the uprights. Then from a subsequent attack on the home goal the defence was penalised for a similar offence. This was taken by the visitors' centre-forward, who shot over the bar. Result :—Mersey Juniors, 2 goals; Penketh Albions, nil. _ _ _ _ _ .

This match ;,as looked *ward to very eagerly, owing to the fact % that Penketh. Only entered. the League last week, and have won their first two engagements against strong teams. . However, on their first visit to War- rin4ton they with with defeat at the hands of Mersey Juniors. The Juniors' forwards missed several shots at an almost open goal. They were, however, 'very tricky, while -their defence always had the Penketh forwards well- in hand.

T. Willoughby played fine for the winners, while the goal scorers were A. Maskell and IT. Smith.

COPPER THEFTS AT WIDNES.

illineer Fined.

At Widnes on Thursday, Victor Horace Osborne, electrical engineer, of Chatham-stroet, Stockport, was charged with thefts of copper from his employers, the British Thomson- Houston Company. The copper was alleged to have been stolen from the Sullivan Chemical Works, Widnes, where:the firm tre erecting an electrical plant, under the prisoner's supervision. There were two charges—one of stealing 1621 b. of copper, valued at 4.10, and other of stealing 421 b., of the value of £26.

Superintendent Foster said that as the result of a visit by a detective to the Widnes marine store it was -found that the prisoner had di' posed of a quantity of cQpper. A box contain.. ing 1621 h. was found at the store, and the police ascertained that Osborne had instructed two men employed under him to take the box to the Widnes Central Station. There, after the men had left, the prisoner met a marine stole dealer, Who conveyed the copper to his store. In the second case 4211 b. of copper was found on the premises of a firm of copper dealers in Manchester, to whom it had been sold by the prisoner.

- Mr. Ernest Howlett, the company's Manches- ter manager, gave evidence as to the firm's custom of disposing of surplus copper, and said that the prisoner had no authority to dispose of the metal.

The accused pleaded that he was responsible for all transactions arising out of the contract upon which he was engaged. He had several outstanding accounts to meet on behalf of the firm, and he intended using the money he had received for the copper to pay those accounts. The prisoner was fined £2O, or two month' imprisonment.

THE LEATHER TRADE.

A Year Of Changing Fortunes And Prospects.

To all intents and purposes the year 1915 may be regarded as at an end, and no doubt many members of the trade are already roughly calcu- lating the results; fortunately, events shaped themselves more happily in this direction than at one time seemed likely, and .while the im- provement came too late to iedeem and average up the heavy falling off which set in during September, it did at least give a degree of con- fidence to the trade which was sorely needed, and made forward buying possible on a some- what considerable scale. I fancy most in the tanning trade will look upon 1915 with mixed feelings; the year cannot be called an alto- gether unsatisfactory one, yet it has contained many disappointments, and in the result will surely fall below the expectations which were formed during the first half. During the latter period trade promised even better returns than before. The demand for all classes of leather was all the time ahead of the production, causing prices to advance sharply. Hides were plentiful, and having regard to the leather market prices were reasonable; tanning materials came along freely, and everything seemed fairly well assured. Towards the middle of the second half a complete change took place, the reasons for which are even not yet clear, and a general falling away in the demand brought about a considerable depreciation in leather values.—The Warrington correspondent. in the "Leather Trades Review."

THE KING'S WISH.

The Recruiting Tribunals will soon be at work hearing appeals. The first meeting of the Advisory Committee, which will deal with the applications of men who have attested and who wish to be released from military service, will hold its first meeting on January 4th.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
Claims and Objections.

In a circular letter to local tribunals the Local Government Board state that when a recom- mendation is made by a Tribunal, a brief state- ment of reasons should be given for the guidance of the Central Appeal Tribunal to whom the recommendation is referred. It is desirable that the recruiting officer or the military representative, if he objects to a cliam or application, should state his reasons, and it will be of great assistance to the Central Appeal Tribunal if the local Tribunal will state whether they consider that the reasons are or are not valid.

An Important Point.

A man is entitled to be treated as starred only if his principal and usual occupation is one of those specifically mentioned, not if he only occasionally does work of the kind covered by one of the occupations. Men With War Badges.

As regards the occupations connected with coal-mining, which are specified in List B, it has been decided that no men engaged in those occupations, whether the men are starred or not, are to be called up for military service except with the consent of the Home Office. It is not neceisarf, therefore, for such men to apply to be starred. Men rightly in possession of official war badges also do not need to make any applica- tion.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
Release of Postal Servants.

It has been arranged with the War Office that no Post Office servant who has been attested under Lord Derby's scheme should be 'called up until the Postmaster-General has intimated that arrangements can be made for his,release. Telegraphists and skilled workmen ine.hided in the revised waiting list for the lt.igal Engineers will not be called up with the groups into which they' fall, but will be retained ita their civil positions until they are required fox enlistment in the 'Signal Service. No Pay Or Travelling Expenses for Tribunals. _ _ _

_ Information has been issued by command of the Army Council with regard to the expPntii- ture which may be chargeable to Army funds in connection with the working of tn. al Tribunals.

The necessity for exercising all possible economy in carrying out this woik at Ihe pre- sent juncture will, it is stated. b? understood, and it is hoped that, to a. ver.v.large extent, the needs of the Tribunals will be met by local authorities and by voluntary 7,flort both in the way of personal- scrvice and pro7isirm Of requirement such as accommodation. Tbus it is anticipated that unless in very sw..:l-.1 ases nr expenditure will be necessary for .hiring looms for meetings, or for the conduct of work, and it is not proposed to admit any hut very exceptional charges under this head. Ex- penditure on furniture or other' capital charges should not, of course. be incurred. Similarly. no par will be admissible for members, nor will travelling expenses be allowed in respect of their attendance at meetings of the Tribunals.

CRUISER SUNK.

Internal Explosion On H.M.S.

Natal.

The Secretary of the Admiralty makes the following announcement through the Press Bureau:—

HAL ship Natal (Captain Eric p. Back, R.N.), armoured cruiser, sunk yesterday afternoon while in harbour as the result of an internal explosion. About 400 survivors are reported, and their names will be communicated to the Press as soon as possible.

MR. JAMES WALSH.

Death Of A Well-known Philomath.

It is with deep regret that we record the death of Mr. James Walsh, only son of Mr. and Mrs. P. Walsh, which took place on Christmas Eve at his residence, 27, Folly-lane, in his thirty-first year. He was taken ill and confined to his bed . about six weeks ago. Engaged as secretary to Mr. 0. Lofthnuse, at the Long- ford Wire Works, he attended to his duties up to the time of his illness. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Wednesday, when a large gathering of friends assembled to pay .their last tribute. The interment ceremony was preceded bye a short service held in St. Paul's Church, where the deceased was a regu- lar worshipper, at which the Rev. E. Downham officiated. The, chief mourners were Mr. P. Walsh (father), Messrs.' D. Walsh and J. Walsh (uncles), Mr. W. C. Harding (brother-in-law), Messrs. R. T. Harding, P. Knowles, A. Keenan. The Warrington Philomathic Society were represented by Messrs. Hedley H. Taylor, D. J. Dunn, N. Nield, J. Ball. A. H. Rathbone and T. Knowles; Longford Wire Works, Messrs. W. Hind, Bladen and W. Lee. Floral tributes were sent by Father and mother. " Alys and Billie," Mr. and Mrs. D. Walsh, Mr. and Mrs. J. Walsh, Mr. and Mrs. R. T. Harding and family. Mr. and Mrs. Hedley H. Taylor, Mr. and Mrs. D. J. Dunn, Mr. and Mrs. N. Nield, Mr. and Mrs, A. H. Rathbone, "Peter and Clara," Mr. C. E. Bunch and Fred, Mrs. John Hankinson and Kathleen, Mrs. J. Cow-

dell. "Old friends at The Folley," Warrington Philornathic Society, Fellow clerks at Longford, Members of the French Society.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
By FELLOW-PHILOMATHS.

In the death of the late Mr. James Walsh the Warrington Philomathic Society has suf- fered an irreparable loss. Throughout the years of his connection with the society his attitude towards its work and ideals has been one of whole-hearted enthusiasm. But his enthusiasm has been all the more effectual in- asmuch as it has been guided by a rare intelli- gence and prompted by a certain fineness of character.

In directing the career of such a society there ,is always a measure of delight and encourage- ment accorded to those who feel that, without fellowship and intellectual strivings, human life is something of a failure; but there are also difficulties to be encountered, disappoint- ments to be borne, flagging. interests to be care- fully watched. No such society ever lives continuously at the summit of its ideals; and there are times when, if it is not to be allowed to descend from a high level of progress, there is need for exceptienal zeal and Sincerity on the part of its, leading members. These per- sonal qualities were amongst the most valued which the late Mr. Walsh brought to bear upon the work of the Philomathic Society. During his presidency, and not legs during the period when he held less distinguished office, he gave unstintingly of the very best that was in him. In the conduct of the society's executive affairs his activities were invaluable. Always tolerant, he was nevertheless immovable whenever any of the society's principles were at stake. In debate, he was stimulating and instructive, and, more, he was chivalrous. Though handi- capped by serious, ill-health, he cheerfully sub- ordinated his personal sufferings to the society's interests: That fact impressed and heartened his fellows. He would have regarded it as a lapse from loyalty, not to have devoted to the society such energies and ability as, by his utmost efforts, he was able to give. It is a privilege and an inspiration to have enjoyed his fellowship. He has passed on, but he will live in the memory or his Philomathic coleagues.

LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COI RT THEATRE.

" Betty " Endears Herself To 'Warrington Audiences.

However sad one might have been this Christmastide, a visit to "Betty," playing to crowded houses, was a sure way to dispel the gloom. This bright and refreshing musical play for months past has been the rage in Lon- don and large provincial towns; and therefore it is no wonder that a visit to Warrington was looked forward to with eagerness and longing. . .

The last Christmas attraction of Messrs. Macdonald and Young's was "The Marriage Market," but in our opinion the present pro- duction is on the high road, to attain even greater popularity than the above-mentioned. Pleased were we to renew old acquaintances, for perusing the programme we noticed old favourites in Miss Dorothy \riggers and Messrs. Myles Clifton and Fred Payne, all of whom played to crowded houses here, Christmas twelve months ago. The • one outstanding feature of " Betty " is the equal division of playing parts which go to make up a performance of high- class merit and competence. Betty, kitchen- maid and lady, is portrayed by Miss Dorothy Hersee in a manner quite in keeping with an already brilliant reputation. The duet, "Dance with me," which she renders with Mr. Haydn Scott, as Gerard, Earl of Beverley, to wbom she is married, is quite ,the hit of the produc- tion, and merited an applause wholehearted and spontaneous. Mr. Myles Clifton, as Lord Playne, nonchalent of manner, is splendid and exceedingly clever. The part comes very natural to him and is rendered in effective style. The humour element is in the capable hands of Mr. Fred Payne, as Achille Jotte, who keeps the audience in one round of merriment and mirth. Mr. Ernest Holloway as the Duke of Crowborough, Mr. W. S.. Stevenson as butler to the Earl of Beverley, are prominent in the cast and acquit themselves admirably, as do Miss Dorothy Viggers as David Playne, Miss Oliva Glynn as Lady Playne, and Miss Yvonne Betts as Chicquette.

• We hope to -have a return visit of "Betty" within the near future, for it is a musical play of which one never can be tired.

"ALADDIN "ARRIVES.

First Pantomime of the. Season in Warrington. The ever-welcome .and popular pantomime " Aladdin," the delight of old and young alike, makes its appearance to Warrington audiences at the Royal Court -Theatre on Monday. next, and judging from the cast, is to be of first-rate quality and excellence. Dainty Dolly Ross is to play the title part with.. Eric Dudley as Abanazar. Teddy Skeam, a comedian of excep- tional note is to portray Aladdin's mother, Widow Twankey, and judging from his past reputation as a mirth raiser, should delight the patrons. Seats may ,be booked at the theatre daily from 10 to 3, and we would advise all intending visitors to journey to this popular house early. There are to be matinees on Monday and Saturday afternoon next at 2.30. The perform- ances on Saturday. January Bth, in the evening, it should be noted, commence at 6.30 and 8.30 respectively.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
lIIGH-CLASS PROGRAMME AT THE

HIPPODROME.

Crowded houses have been the rule at the Hippodrome this week, which is only natural, as we have had one of the best of programmes that have recently been presented at this popu- lar place of amusement. The chief item is a sketch entitled "'Listing for a Soldier," in which Mr. J. J. Wild's company appears. The sketch is one that creates roars of laughter by the superb acting of the principal characters, who have mastered the art of speaking the Lancashire dialect. It concludes with a note of pathos which shows that "things are not what they seem," and that there isimany sound heart beneath a rough exterior. The Four its provide a good entertainment with their clever dancing and vivacious singing. A "turn" very much out of the ordinary is that supplied by the Four Sisters Ruby, who are acrobats and dancers of no mean ability. George Ross is a musician that can play the piano from almost any position, and his per- formance is marked throughout by an evtra- ordinary liveliness. A number Of fine selections on the violin are played by Doraswami, whilst the Four Shades excellently render a number of good quartet items. E. G. Swan is a comedian with a style of his own, and his mimicry and songs are good.

Next Week's Special Fare.

The management have scfmred many turns of considerable note and ability to begin the New Year, for old favourites will be seen in Scott and Whaley, eccentric coloured comedians, who occupy premier position on a splendid bill. Ed. J. Huxter is to present " Inebfiate Revels," and will, we believe, be worth witnessing. The Reece Duo, musicians; Hinsle, comedy illusion- ist; Hall and Turn, comedy acrobats; and Gyto, described as a jocular juggler, are also booked to appear. The Three Curtis's, in .song S and dances—past and present—is a "turn" in itself worth the price of admission.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD ALL-ROUND PROGRAMME AT TIIE STAR KINEMA.

A fine love-drama entitled "May Blossom," occupied premier position at the Star Kinema during the first part of the week. It was shown in four parts and was hugely enjoyed by crowded houses each day. A feature of the picture were the beautiful landscapes. A further episode of that magnificent picture "The Exploits of Elaine," featuring the "Double Trap," proved to be very thrilling, especially the struggle on the church spire between Kennedy the detective and one of the " Clutch- ing Hand's" accomplices. Good war scenes are depicted in the " Pathe Gazette," while "Sister Susie Sewing Shirts for Soldiers " Proved to be a very laughable picture. _ _ _

For the last three days the " star " Picture is entitled " Niche."

NEW YEAR—NEW FILMS. Excellent Pictures Booked For Next Week's Prcgramme.

The Star Kinema, that popular cinema house in Church-street, has by the excellence of its pictures during the past year become one of the foremost, if not the foremost, rendezvous for lovers of " movies " in the town, and the management are determined to leave no stone upturned to keep up their reputation during the New Year. Two splendid top bill 'films are to be shown next week in "Such a Good Little Devil" and "ale Wild Goose Chase" respectively. Other noteworthy films by cele- brated companies are to be screened, and under no circumstances must they be missed, for the programme is one of the best and will be thoroughly enjoyable.

GREAT CIRCUS AT OLYMPIA

The Christmas programme of the Liverpool Olympia was opened this week, to a house abso- lutely crowded in every part. This was only t 6 be expected in view of the high degree of excel- lence of the talent provided. A circus has been found in previous years to be a striking holiday attraction, and,no effort has been spared again to present an abundance and variety, of circus fare which would please the most fastidious or expectant. In addition there is a feature which in itself could at ordinary times be regarded as a full bill in the special engagementaf the band of the Coldstream Guards, as fine a combination of regimental musicians as anyone could wish to hear. They led off the programme with a fine selection of patriotic airs, played with such fire and feeling as to stir the blood. The selected overture was also played with much taste, and particularly by the reed instruments and horns with genuine skill. T 4 musical pro. gramme., it may be added, is changed for each performance. ,

. , With regard to the circus itself,' the per- formers are all Allies—the nationality, British. French, Italian, &c., being marked on the pro- gramme. • and in the one case where our own friends in battle are not represented, the lady being a Swede, it is worth noting that her horses are Russian. One of the most popular turns is that provided by Petite Nina and her motor. cycling sea lions, which give a really marvellous performance, their balancing feats being won- RossVs musical elephants also givc

Gigantic Attractions.

The "Great Circus" will be continued again next week, and will, be as good as ever. Per- formances will be given at 2 and 7 daily, With the exception of Saturday, when they will be, at 2, 6.30 and 8.50. The band of the Coldstream .Guards will continue to render selections.

WARRINGTON GUARDIANS.

Suggested War-Time Economies

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday morning,. Mr. A. J. Jolley being in the chair.. • A lengthy communication was read from Mr. Walter 'Long (the President •of the Local: Government Board), suggesting the curtailment of clerical work under the Guardians. One of: the. suggestions was that monthly meetings of. the :Guardians should be substituted for fort- nightly meetings during the period of the war. Mr. Mather: That has been my opinion for - some time.

The suggestions on the circular will be in- vestigated by Messrs. A. J. Jolley (chairman), W. Furness (vice-chairman) and A. Bottomley (clerk), and a report will be submitted at the next iniling.

===== 0002601_19160101_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
AN AGREEMENT REACHED.

Mr: Billinge was to have moved "That the minute of the Board dated December Ist, 1915,. authorising the Clerk to take legal proceeding& against the .Newton-in-Makerfield overseers, be• rescinded."

At a recent meeting the clerk was instructed to take legal proceedings l*ause the Newton, overseers refused to pay their calls. But the. Newton overseers have now forwarded the cheque. and Mr. Billinge withdrew his motion, as there is no Aecessity to take the proceedings..

Letters. From Former Pupils Of The Pacfgate Cottage Homes.

At a meeting of the Warrington Guardians On Wednesday, letters of thanks were read from two former pupils at the Padgate • Cottage Homes. •

A boy named Walter Brimilow, who is now at St. Vincent's Roman Catholic Home, Fut- wood, Pre.,ton, wrote:—

"1 have the greatest pleasure in writing my first letter to you. (the Chainhan) and the other Cluardians. to wish you a very happy Christmas and every happiness and success in the New Year. All the Warrington boys unite in my good wishes, and would like you to know that; they are very happy here and quite well. I hope all the l'adgate boys will enjoy them - ',Ayes as usual at Christmas."

A similar letter Avis read, whieh had been signed by ten girls, who are at the Moorfield Convent, Ribbleton-avenue, Preston. One of the sentences read: "The children have not forgotten you. We cannot forget you because you have .been so kind to us. We will offer up our Communion for you when we go to Midnight Mass."

The letters Nrere referred to the Padgate Cot- tage Homes Committee for acknowledgment.

Uncle Jack, " Examiner," Warrington. THE OLD YEAR AND THE NEW. MY DEAR CHILDREN,— ' The competition in which you •%ere asked to explain your ideas as to the year that finished yesterd'ay and the year that begins to-day has not brought so many answers as it might have done, but perhaps my little people have, as one of them says, found the. subject rather difficult. It is a 'big subject, butcnot so difficult. when tackled in the right spirit. •Taken on the whole, the letter that comes from Harold Whittle, 123, Cartwright-street; Warrington, is worthy of the prize, and I adjudged him the winner. , Mistakes to be Avoided. He writes:— . The Old Year and the New Year.—When I look back on the old year I see many mistakes I have made; many little_kindnesseS .1 could have done which seem more 'unkind to look back upon.' In the coming' year I linve made up my mind to be. kinder towards everybody in small things as Well as big things. I will, in the New Year, tty to be obedient towards my mother and father and all that are older than myself. I will -finish my letter by _ wishing- you a prosperous _and happy New Year. The same to -Uncle Ned.—Your nephew, HAROLD. This is not the first time Harold has won- the prize. "I was sorry," he tells me, "that you did not receive my thanks for the very nice book I got for the essay, on ..` My Favourite Game.' • I gave the paper to one of your other nephews to put in your letter box, but he forgot it for a week." I am sorry, too, for I. like my nephews and nieces to show their appreciation, and when I hear nothing of the prizes after they have left my hands lam saddened. I don't like ungrate- ful boys and girls. Besides, unless I am told I don't know whether the prizes have reached their owners. Do you know a few—only. a few I am glad'to flaw: ac:r.iiowledged the

Sergeant Tom Widd, of the Roan Cow Inn, Bridge Foot, is one of the old Volunteers, and went out in February with the Warrington Territorials. He is twenty-five years of age, and has been associated with the Volunteers and Territorials for a matter of nearly ten years. Well-known locally as a footballer and athlete, Sergeant Widd's chief line is rearing and selling poultry. He has managed the affairs of various poultry firms with great skill and splendid results, both from the prize- winning and general business point of view. CORPORAL J. A. VALE. was wounded on December 11th, while serving with the Warrington Territorials. He is Private Alfred Cooper, and at present he is in hospital at Filsham Park, St. Leonards-on-Sea, suffer- ing from a broken left arm, caused by a piece of shell. He has written home to say that he is progressing very favourably. • Private Cooper, who is 23 years of age, as a boy attended Christ Church School. Latch- ford, and previous to enlisting over twelve months ago, was employed by the Whitecross Co., Ltd. Mr. Cooper's other sons who are serving are: Sergt-Major William Arthur Cooper, of the Ist Battalion South Lancashire Regimvt, who is a single man, 31 years of age, and who has been in the Army about ten years; Private Frank Cooper, of the 2/4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, who is in training in the South, is a married man, and resides at 22. Cumberland-street, • Latchford; Private Fred Cooper, of the 2nd/4th Battalion South Lanca- shire Regiment, is 19 years of age, and was educated at Christ Church, Latchford. He *as formerly employed by the London ad Railway Co. He also is in training.

Iron Carted Away In Cinders From • Rylands'S Yard. At the Borough Mice Court on Thursday. before Mr. W. Roberts (in the chair), Messrs.. A. J. Jolley and Dr. Naden, two carters in the employ of a local contractor were charged with stealing two cwt. of scrap iron, value :55., from the wo;ks. of 'Rylands- Bros., Ltd., Church-street: The accused were William. IToult, IsherWood's, Yard, Church-Street, and Thomas Clarke, 'll, Howard-street. The Chief Constable said that on Wednesday P.C. Yost was in the vicinity of Rylands's works, when he observed on a cart driven by-one of the defendants a quantity of scrap iron. From fur- ther inquiries it was found that the iron had been taken from the works of Messrs. Rylands. The men for a week or so had been engaged cart- ing cinders from the works to the Howley tip. When in the works the carters had to pass the scrap iron heap. It appears' that the two pris- oners had arranged to take the iron to the stables and there conceaj it until there was suffi- cient for disposal, and tile proceeds to be divided between them. . Hoult, in a statement at the Police Station, stated that it was on the suggestion of Clarke that they arranged to take the iron. Clarke, who had been twice previously con- vieted: for larceny, was fined Ms., and'Hoult 40s„ or in default 28 days each. •

WARRINGTON FOOTBALLERS IN THE ARMY. Before, the opening of the present football .season the Warrington officials decided to release their players for the duration of the war. A good number of them have taken this opportunity,„and7the record of Warrington will be hard to'beat. Taking the two teams together, we find that the following have joined the. Forces:—J.. W. Chester, G. A. Skelhorn; A. Bradshaw, Thomas, S. Nicholas, Harold Davies,.G.•Dicken- Son, T. Steel, A. Brown, D. Bevan. T. Broad- hurst, Howard' . Davies, W. a. 3forgan, A. Naylor, J. Woyivell, T. W. Clarke, G.- Shep- herd, F. Wright, W. Cunliffe, J. E. Critchley, W: H. Blundell and W. Powncey. Many juniors who were on. the Warrington League register have also joined. Old Players Killed and Wounded. Broadhurst and Howard Davies have been killed in action, and Skelhorn is on the Terri- torial .wounded list. Jack Fish and R. Unsworth are among the old, players who have enlisted. Most of the Association teams in Warrington have been depleted of men, the result of which is seen in the fact that the League is in a very poor way, and as in Rugby. the game is really surviving by the activities of the juniors.

That there are Sales AND Sales we readily admit but that a Sale with us is a GENUINE SALE and a THOROUGHLY HONEST BARGAIN EVENT is nothing less than an article of implicit faith with every Lady in the community. There are no " ifs and " buts about the forthcoming Sale and when we say that EVERYTHING WILL BE REDUCED we mean what we say. It therefore follows as the night the day that the purchasing power of Pounds, Shillings and Pence will be infinitely greater than before especially in view of the fact that QUALITY remains unimpaired and en- tirely unaffected by the sudden drop in price. We ask you, Madame, in your own interest to visit our Sale on the opening day and to repeat that visit as often as you will throughout the whole Sale period.
